The single source for the fill characters is `FigurativeConstants.FillChar`. (A BOOLEAN receiver is outside this item: ZERO fills it with the boolean character '0', and so does SPACE, determination D-B2 in §3.) | `conformance:85/l1c10_figurative_space_quote_zero_chars`; `conformance:2002/l1c10_figurative_code_values` |
| DOC-A.1-76 | **File sharing — which devices allow concurrent access to a physical file**, §9.1.15, required + documented | **All of them.** Every physical file is a path in the host file system: a regular file, or a device path such as `/dev/null` that the host opens as a file. The device classes `DISK` and `PRINTER` both name such a file (item 71). WiseOwl COBOL treats every physical file as one that allows concurrent access, so it is *shareable* in the sense of §14.9.27.4 GR21 and the SHARING clause and SHARING phrase always apply. A second connector's OPEN of the same file is decided by §9.1.15 Table 19: connectors that both specify `SHARING WITH ALL OTHER` (with `LOCK MODE MANUAL`) can be open on one regular file or on `/dev/null` at the same time, each with status 00, and a `SHARING WITH NO OTHER` OPEN of a file another connector has open fails with 61. `ASSIGN TO PRINTER "f"` and `ASSIGN TO DISK "f"` are the same shareable file. This row covers only which files can be shared. How the file lock excludes other run units and other languages is item 75 (open on Linux and macOS under kb/Work PB833). The mode used when no sharing mode is specified is item 77. Implemented by `FileRegistry.SharedOpenAttempt` and `PhysicalFileTable`, which apply Table 19 to every connector without testing the device. | `conformance:2002/l1c11_sharing_every_device_shareable` |
| DOC-A.1-78 | **Fixed file attributes — whether the ability to share a physical file is a fixed file attribute**, §9.1.6, required + documented | **No.** A physical file records nothing about sharing. The sharing mode belongs to each FILE CONNECTOR: it comes from that connector's SHARING clause, or from the SHARING phrase of the OPEN that opens it (§14.9.27.4 GR21–GR23). It is set when the OPEN succeeds and checked against the connectors that have the same physical file open at that moment (§9.1.15 Table 19). When the connector is closed, its sharing mode is gone. So a file created through a `SHARING WITH NO OTHER` connector can later be opened through two `SHARING WITH ALL OTHER` connectors at once, and both OPENs succeed with 00. While the NO OTHER connector is open, any other OPEN of the file fails with 61, and the NO OTHER connector's own OPEN fails with 61 while other connectors have the file open. This is also why A.1 item 129's validated fixed attributes never include sharing. Implemented by `FileRegistry.RegisterSharing` (the per-connector mode) and `FileRegistry.SharedOpenAttempt` (the check at OPEN). | `conformance:2002/l1c11_sharing_not_fixed_attribute` |
| DOC-A.1-79 | **FLAG-02 directive — the warning mechanism**, §7.3.14 GR1 (§7.3.14.4 GR1), required + documented | **A compile-time warning, COBOLNET1620, for each flagged construct. The warning does not change the compiled program.** `>>FLAG-02` names ALL, or one or more of its five options (EC-PROGRAM-EXCEPTIONS, I-O-STATUS-07, MOVE-TO-SAME-NAME, RANGE-EXCEPTION-FOR-INDEX, TERMINATE-WITH-VARYING) in any order, followed by ON or OFF. Both pairs of braces carry a default, because the printed format leaves ALL and ON un-underlined (optional words, §5.2.3; the default alternative, §5.2.6.3): with no option named ALL applies, and with neither ON nor OFF written ON applies, so `>>FLAG-02`, `>>FLAG-02 ON` and `>>FLAG-02 OFF` are legal. An option is on from the line after the directive until a later FLAG-02 turns it off, or turns ALL off, or the compilation group ends. Every option is off by default. While an option is on, each construct §7.3.14.4 GR4 describes for it is reported as `warning COBOLNET1620: <the construct> (<the 2002-to-2014 change>) — flagged by >>FLAG-02 <option>` at the construct's line and column. For example, with I-O-STATUS-07 on, each `CLOSE ... WITH NO REWIND` or `UNIT` is reported. An option word that is not one of the five, or ALL combined with named options, or an option word or ALL written twice (§5.2.6.4), or an ON or OFF that is not the last word, is refused with COBOLNET1622. The directive is a COBOL-2014 element: at `--std 2002` and below it is refused with COBOLNET0900, and at `--std 2023` it still works but draws the obsolete-element warning COBOLNET0903 (§7.3.14.1 NOTE). Known defect in the open notes, not part of this determination: an OO method body's operands are not name-resolved, so MOVE-TO-SAME-NAME and RANGE-EXCEPTION-FOR-INDEX flag nothing inside a method (an advisory false negative, never a false warning). EC-PROGRAM-EXCEPTIONS flags each qualifying `>>TURN` at its own line when the SOURCE ELEMENT it belongs to (§3.164: a program, a function, a contained program, a class, an interface or a method, excluding what it contains) calls a function (including a function-identifier written without the word FUNCTION, and one inside a subscript) or invokes a method (INVOKE, an inline method invocation, an object-property reference); a `>>TURN` written before an element's IDENTIFICATION DIVISION belongs to the element that follows it, and a CALL of a program is not a trigger. MOVE-TO-SAME-NAME treats a reference-modified operand as category alphanumeric (§8.4.3.3.4 GR6 a), so only the subordinate OCCURS DEPENDING arm flags it. TERMINATE-WITH-VARYING reads a VARYING clause on any report group description entry. Implemented by `FlagDirectiveLine.TryParse`, `FlagState` and `FlagConformancePass`; the diagnostic is `DiagnosticCatalog.Flag02Warning`. | `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Compile_Flag02IoStatus07On_EmitsCobolnet1620_OnEachPhraseSpelling`; `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Parse_Flag02_OnIsImplicit_WhenOnOffOmitted`; `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Parse_AllAndOn_AreTheDefaults_WhenOmitted`; `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Parse_RepeatedAlternative_IsError`; `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Compile_Flag02_DefaultedBraces_SelectTheRightOptions`; `conformance:2014/pb1374_flag02_defaults`; `conformance:negative/pb1374-flag02-all-repeated`; `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Fold_DefaultsOff_AndIsStickyForwardStrictlyAfterTheDirective`; `unit:FlagPopulationTests.EcProgram_EachQualifyingTurnIsFlaggedAtItsOwnLine` |
| DOC-A.1-80 | **FLAG-14 directive — the warning mechanism**, §7.3.15 GR1 (§7.3.15.4 GR1), required + documented | **A compile-time warning, COBOLNET1621, for each flagged construct. The warning does not change the compiled program.** `>>FLAG-14` names ALL, or one or more of its twelve options (COMPILE-TIME-ARITHMETIC-EXPRESSIONS, EVALUATE, I-O-DECLARATIVE, I-O-STATUS-04, I-O-STATUS-07, NUM-ED-ZERO-FIGCONST, READ-PREVIOUS, REF-MOD-ZERO-LENGTH, VALUE-EDITING, VALUE-FIG-CON-LENGTH, VALUE-ZERO, WRITE-END-OF-PAGE) in any order, followed by ON or OFF. Both pairs of braces carry a default, because the printed format leaves ALL and ON un-underlined (optional words, §5.2.3; the default alternative, §5.2.6.3): with no option named ALL applies, and with neither ON nor OFF written ON applies, so `>>FLAG-14 VALUE-ZERO`, `>>FLAG-14 ALL`, `>>FLAG-14 ON`, `>>FLAG-14 OFF` and a bare `>>FLAG-14` are legal. An option is on from the line after the directive until a later FLAG-14 turns it off, or turns ALL off, or the compilation group ends. Every option is off by default. While an option is on, each construct §7.3.15.4 GR4 describes for it is reported as `warning COBOLNET1621: <the construct> (<the 2014-to-2023 change>) — flagged by >>FLAG-14 <option>` at the construct's line and column. For example, `>>FLAG-14 READ-PREVIOUS I-O-STATUS-04 ON` reports each READ PREVIOUS statement and each reference that tests a FILE STATUS item for '04', and `>>FLAG-14 VALUE-ZERO ON` reports a numeric-edited item whose VALUE is ZERO. An option word that is not one of the twelve, or ALL combined with named options, or an option word or ALL written twice (§5.2.6.4), or an ON or OFF that is not the last word, is refused with COBOLNET1622. The directive is COBOL-2023 only: at `--std 2014` and below it is refused with COBOLNET0900. Known defect in the open notes, not part of this determination: an OO method body's operands are not name-resolved (an advisory false negative). Each option flags its GR4 population: COMPILE-TIME-ARITHMETIC-EXPRESSIONS flags an expression with an arithmetic operator in a DEFINE, EVALUATE or DISPLAY directive, a constant conditional expression and a CONSTANT entry; I-O-DECLARATIVE flags a statement that can take an INVALID KEY phrase (not a REWRITE of a relative file in sequential access, §14.9.35.3 SR2, nor a DELETE RECORD of a file in sequential access, §14.9.10.3 SR2); I-O-STATUS-04 and I-O-STATUS-07 flag a relation, an abbreviated combined relation, a level-88 condition-name and an EVALUATE selection subject/object pair that tests the FILE STATUS item; NUM-ED-ZERO-FIGCONST, VALUE-EDITING and VALUE-ZERO treat a numeric item with BLANK WHEN ZERO as numeric-edited (§13.18.8.4 GR2); WRITE-END-OF-PAGE flags a WRITE that does not specify the AT END-OF-PAGE phrase, one with only NOT AT END-OF-PAGE included (§14.9.51.3 SR19). Implemented by `FlagDirectiveLine.TryParse`, `FlagState` and `FlagConformancePass`; the diagnostic is `DiagnosticCatalog.Flag14Warning`. | `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Compile_Flag14ReadPreviousOn_EmitsCobolnet1621`; `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Parse_AllAndOn_AreTheDefaults_WhenOmitted`; `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Parse_RepeatedAlternative_IsError`; `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Compile_Flag14_DefaultedBraces_SelectTheRightOptions`; `conformance:2023/pb1374_flag14_defaults`; `conformance:negative/pb1374-flag14-option-repeated`; `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Compile_Flag14ReadPreviousOff_EmitsNoFlagWarning`; `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Fold_DefaultsOff_AndIsStickyForwardStrictlyAfterTheDirective`; `unit:FlagPopulationTests.IoStatus_FlagsEveryConditionThatTestsTheFileStatusItem` |
| DOC-A.1-94 | **GOBACK statement — constraints on the value in the STATUS phrase**, §14.9.18.3 GR10 (printed as §14.9.18.4 GR10 in this text), required + documented | **No value is refused. The STATUS operand becomes the process exit code**, with the same mapping as STOP (A.1 item 192, §3 'STOP RUN / GOBACK termination status'). The syntax rules still apply: identifier-2 must be an integer data item or a data item of usage display or national (§14.9.18.3 SR6), and a numeric literal-1 must be an integer (SR7). The mapping is: **(1)** a numeric operand's integer value is the exit code (a data item's value truncated toward zero); **(2)** a non-numeric operand (an alphanumeric or national item, a non-numeric literal, a figurative constant) is decoded as an unsigned integer from its digit characters, and a position that is not a digit adds no digit, so `STATUS "007"` exits 7, `STATUS "A1B2"` exits 12 and `STATUS "ABEND"` or `STATUS SPACE` exits 0 (`CobolNum.FromAlphanumeric`); **(3)** the host then keeps what it can report: the value is clamped (never wrapped) to the 32-bit range, so `STATUS 4294967296` exits 2,147,483,647 (kb/Work PB1178), and a POSIX host keeps only the low 8 bits, so `STATUS 300` exits 44 and `STATUS -3` exits 253. With no STATUS value, `GOBACK WITH ERROR` exits 1 and `WITH NORMAL` (or no phrase) exits 0; a STATUS value, when present, wins over ERROR or NORMAL. The value reaches the operating system only when the GOBACK ends the run unit, that is, in a main program (§14.9.18.4 GR3); in a called program the GOBACK returns to its caller and the status is not used. The phrase is COBOL-2023 only: at `--std 2014` and below it is refused with COBOLNET0900. Implemented by `CallBinder.DecodeGobackPhrases` and the `RunUnit.ExitStatus` setter. | `conformance-test:StopGobackExitCodeTests.TerminationStatus_SetsProcessExitCode`; `conformance-test:StopGobackExitCodeTests.NonNumericStatusOperand_IsLegalAndInterpretedNumerically` |
| DOC-A.1-95 | **Hexadecimal alphanumeric literals — result when no corresponding character exists**, §8.3.3.2 GR4 (printed as §8.3.3.2.4 GR5 in this text), required + documented | **The case does not arise: every hex-character-sequence has a corresponding character.** A hexadecimal alphanumeric literal takes TWO hexadecimal digits per character (§8.3.3.2.3 SR6; any other count is refused, COBOLNET1635), and the group `hh` is the character whose code is U+00hh, which is also its one-byte DISPLAY image (item 209). All 256 values 00 through FF are characters of the alphanumeric set, so each group is stored as that character, including the control codes 00–1F, 7F and 80–9F, with no diagnostic and no exception condition. Example: `X"80FF0041"` is four characters whose `FUNCTION ORD` values are 129, 256, 1 and 66. What a device shows for a control character on DISPLAY is the device's business, not the literal's. Implemented in `CobolLiteral.DecodeHex`. | `conformance:2002/l1c12_hex_alphanumeric_every_group` |
| DOC-A.1-97 | **Hexadecimal national literals — result when no corresponding character exists**, §8.3.3.5 GR4 (printed as §8.3.3.5.4 GR5 in this text), required + documented | A hexadecimal national literal takes FOUR hexadecimal digits per character (§8.3.3.5.3 SR5; any other count is refused, COBOLNET1635), and each group is stored **verbatim as that UTF-16 code unit**, the national character representation (item 33). A group that is not a character on its own, such as a surrogate half D800–DFFF or a noncharacter such as FFFE or FFFF, is stored unchanged, as one character position, with no diagnostic and no exception condition. It then behaves as any other national character: `FUNCTION LENGTH(NX"D800")` is 1, `FUNCTION ORD` gives the code plus 1 (55297 for D800, 65536 for FFFF), and comparison uses the code unit's value. Two groups that form a valid surrogate pair stay two character positions. On DISPLAY to a UTF-8 device, an unpaired surrogate is written as U+FFFD. Implemented in `CobolLiteral.DecodeHexGroups`. | `conformance:2002/l1c12_hex_national_no_character` |
| DOC-A.1-100 | **Implementor-defined level-2 exception conditions, EC-level-2-IMP — specification and meaning of each specified level-2 IMP exception condition**, §14.6.13.1.6 Table 13 (§14.6.13.1.1, the '-IMP' suffix type), optional + documented if provided | **Provided: 20 of Table 13's 22 EC-level-2-IMP names are valid exception-names, and each is FATAL.** Table 13's 22 names are EC-ARGUMENT-IMP, EC-BOUND-IMP, EC-CONTINUE-IMP, EC-DATA-IMP, EC-EXTERNAL-IMP, EC-FLOW-IMP, EC-FUNCTION-IMP, EC-I-O-IMP, EC-LOCALE-IMP, EC-MCS-IMP, EC-OO-IMP, EC-ORDER-IMP, EC-OVERFLOW-IMP, EC-PROGRAM-IMP, EC-RAISING-IMP, EC-RANGE-IMP, EC-REPORT-IMP, EC-SCREEN-IMP, EC-SIZE-IMP, EC-SORT-MERGE-IMP, EC-STORAGE-IMP and EC-VALIDATE-IMP. EC-CONTINUE-IMP, EC-EXTERNAL-IMP and EC-MCS-IMP are COBOL-2023 names; below 2023 they are refused with COBOLNET0878. EC-SCREEN-IMP and EC-VALIDATE-IMP belong to the screen and VALIDATE modules, which this implementation does not provide (Annex A.4.2, A.4.14). They are refused wherever they are written, with COBOLNET1707 and COBOLNET1710. The other 20 can be used in `>>TURN`, `RAISE EXCEPTION` and wherever an exception-name is allowed. Table 13 leaves their fatality to the implementor (Imp), and this implementation treats every one as fatal. An unhandled one ends the run unit abnormally, and so does a `RAISE EXCEPTION` of one with checking not enabled. **Meaning: the implementation itself sets only EC-PROGRAM-IMP**, and only when a contained program is activated while the program that contains it has no instance (an internal consistency failure of the program table, reported as 'internal: contained program ... activated while its container is not instantiated'). The implementation never sets any of the others, so a program sees them only when it raises them itself. EC-I-O-IMP is Table 13's 'I-O status 9x' condition, but the only 9x status WiseOwl COBOL defines, '90', carries EC-I-O-LINAGE instead (item 110). EC-RAISING-IMP is never set at run time either. The names and fatality are `ExceptionCatalog` (EcFatality.Imp); the one raise site is `ProgramTable.ParentInstance`, through the default exception-name of `CobolCallException`. A conforming program does not reach that site: it guards an internal inconsistency, because the §8.4.6.3 scope rules admit a caller of a contained program only from inside its container's own subtree, and that caller's activation keeps the container instantiated. Every other level-2 `-IMP` name is raised only by the program's own `RAISE EXCEPTION`. The name list above is held equal to the catalog by `EcImplementorNamesDocumentationDriftTests`. | `conformance-test:FatalRaiseSelectionTests.RaiseLevel2ImpName_Provided_TerminatesAbnormally`; `unit:EcImplementorNamesDocumentationDriftTests.Item100_NamesExactlyTheCatalogsImplementorDefinedLevel2Names`; `conformance:2002/l1_dns_a1_99_ec_imp_level2_valid` |
| DOC-A.1-101 | **INVOKE statement — behavior when invoking a non-COBOL method**, §14.9.23.4 GR2 b) (and the GR7 e) execution of a non-COBOL method), required + documented | **A non-COBOL method is never invoked: WiseOwl COBOL provides no way to designate one, so every INVOKE reaches a method written in COBOL or fails.** Every method an INVOKE can reach is declared in a COBOL class definition of the compilation group. A REPOSITORY class specifier whose externalized name is a .NET type (`CLASS SB AS "System.Text.StringBuilder"`) does not bind to that type: `INVOKE SB "NEW" …` is the compile-time error COBOLNET0823 (`OoBinder.OoBindInvoke`), and naming it in `USAGE OBJECT REFERENCE SB` is COBOLNET0813. The members every .NET object carries (`ToString`, `GetHashCode`, `Equals`, …) are not methods of a COBOL object: on a typed receiver `INVOKE T "ToString"` is the compile-time error COBOLNET0825 (§14.9.23.3 SR4 b)); on a universal object reference, or with the method-name held in a data item, the runtime dispatch `CobolObject.__CobolInvoke` searches only the COBOL-declared methods of the object's class hierarchy, finds none, and sets **EC-OO-METHOD** (fatal, §14.9.23.4 GR7 b)), so an unhandled occurrence terminates the run unit. GR7 e)'s implementor-defined execution of a non-COBOL method therefore never occurs. COBOL-2002, 2014 and 2023 (COBOL-85 has no INVOKE). How a REPOSITORY class specifier that names no definition of the compilation group is resolved is open under kb/Work PB1086. | `conformance:2002/l1_invoke_dotnet_member_universal`; `conformance:negative/l1-invoke-dotnet-class-new`; `conformance:negative/l1-invoke-dotnet-member-typed` |
| DOC-A.1-104 | **I-O status — the value placed when more than one value applies**, §9.1.13.1, required — A.1 says the internal procedure need not be documented, and it is documented here anyway so that a program can rely on the value it tests | **Exactly one I-O status value is placed per execution of an input-output statement, and the choice is fixed, never timing-dependent.** (1) **An unsuccessful value always wins over a successful one**: a successful-completion warning ('0x') never displaces a status that begins with another digit (`FileRegistry.NoRewindPhraseEffect` tests the first digit). (2) **Among unsuccessful values, the statement's preconditions are tested in the order its general rules state them, and the first one that fails supplies the status**: for a sequential READ, the open mode ('47') first, then a preceding unsuccessful READ or START ('46'), then the first READ of an absent OPTIONAL file ('10'), then a record locked by another file connector ('51'), and only then the physical retrieval (`FileConnector.SequentialReadGuard`). So a failing READ keeps its '47', '46' or '10' rather than reporting a '51' for a record it would never have read. For a REWRITE or a DELETE RECORD, the open mode ('49', §14.9.35.4 GR3 / §14.9.10.4 GR1) is tested before a record locked by another file connector ('51', GR11 / GR6), so a connector that is not open in the I-O mode — closed, or open INPUT — answers '49' even while another connector holds the record it names (`FileConnector.MutationOpenModeGuard`; kb/Work PB1194). (3) **Between two successful values, a value the statement's own general rules assign wins over a value only described in §9.1.13.2**: an `OPEN INPUT … WITH NO REWIND` of an OPTIONAL file that is not present reports **'07'** (§14.9.27.4 GR11), not '05' (§9.1.13.2 item 4 a)). The first READ of that file still reports the at-end '10', so the information '05' would have carried is not lost. Same in every edition. | `conformance:2023/pb317_open_no_rewind`; `conformance:2002/w73b_pb1194_mutation_open_mode_first` |
| DOC-A.1-106 | **I-O status 0x — the value of x**, §9.1.13.3 item 1, optional (documented if provided) | **Not provided.** No input-output statement ever sets an I-O status of '0' followed by a letter: the successful-completion values WiseOwl COBOL produces are the standard's '00', '02', '04', '05', '06', '07' and '09' (`FileStatusCode`), and its implementor-defined I-O statuses are the fatal '90' and '91' (item 110). Because no '0x' value exists, the COBOL-2023 latitude on whether an upper-case and a lower-case x are equivalent does not arise. | `conformance:2023/l1_dns_a1_106_no_0x_io_status` |
| DOC-A.1-111 | **LEAP-SECOND directive — whether a value greater than 59 seconds may be reported, and the maximum**, §7.3.17.4 GR2 (with §14.9.1.4 GR11, §15.21, §15.99), required + documented | **NO — the seconds position never holds a value greater than 59, with `>>LEAP-SECOND ON` or OFF, so the maximum is 59.** Every reported time comes from the one run-unit clock (`RunUnit.Current.Clock`, a .NET `DateTimeOffset`, which cannot represent a leap second): `ACCEPT … FROM TIME` (`AcceptSource.Time`, seconds in positions 5–6 of HHMMSScc), `FUNCTION CURRENT-DATE` (`CobolDate.CurrentDate`) and, from COBOL-2014, `FUNCTION FORMATTED-CURRENT-DATE` (`CobolDate.FormattedCurrentDate`), and `FUNCTION WHEN-COMPILED`, whose stamp is the compile-time clock reading baked into the program (`ProgramEmitter`). `>>LEAP-SECOND ON` is recognized from COBOL-2002 on (`LeapSecondDirectiveProcessor`) and changes only the ARGUMENT side of the date and time functions, where a seconds value of 60 is accepted — see item 112, which also records that SECONDS-PAST-MIDNIGHT never returns 86,400 or more. Pinned at the one instant where a 60 could arise, the last tick before the 2016-12-31 leap second (23:59:59.9999999 UTC), with ON specified, ON implied by a bare `>>LEAP-SECOND`, OFF specified and OFF implied: the run-time sources through the `COBOLNET_CLOCK` seam (`LeapSecondReportedSecondsPinTests`, at 2002 and 2023) and the WHEN-COMPILED bake through `IntrinsicBinder.CompileClock` (`WhenCompiledStampTests.WhenCompiled_LeapSecond*`), every one expecting second 59 with hundredths 99. The live-clock goldens `l1g4_leap_second_on_reported_seconds`, `l1g4_leap_second_off_reported_seconds` and `l1g4_leap_second_off_explicit_2002` are layout witnesses only. | `conformance-test:LeapSecondReportedSecondsPinTests.ReportedSeconds_On_LastTickBeforeLeapSecond_NeverExceeds59`; `conformance-test:LeapSecondReportedSecondsPinTests.ReportedSeconds_ImpliedOn_BareDirective_LastTickBeforeLeapSecond_NeverExceeds59`; `unit:WhenCompiledStampTests.WhenCompiled_LeapSecondOn_LastTickBeforeLeapSecond_Bakes59`; `unit:WhenCompiledStampTests.WhenCompiled_LeapSecondImpliedOn_BareDirective_LastTickBeforeLeapSecond_Bakes59` |
| DOC-A.1-117 | **Listings — whether and when produced by the compiler, and the effect of logical conversion**, §7.3.18.3 GR1 + §6.5 (Clause 7), required + documented | **The compiler NEVER produces a source listing**, in any edition and under any option: the `cobol` command has no listing option, and a compilation writes only the assembly, its runtime configuration and the generated C# source (`<name>.g.cs`), which is not a listing of the COBOL source. Diagnostics go to standard error and identify the source line and column. Because there is no listing, **`>>LISTING` (ON, OFF, or with no operand) and `>>PAGE` are accepted and have no effect** (§7.3.18.3 GR1; §7.3.19): they are syntax-checked and hold no state (`DirectiveStateRegistry`, NONE HELD), and they compile from COBOL-2002 on. Like every `>>` directive they are rejected at COBOL-85 with COBOLNET0900. **Logical conversion (§6.5) has no effect on a listing**, because no listing exists. | `conformance:2002/pb725_directive_facility` |
| DOC-A.1-118 | **Locale specification — how the user default and system default locales are defined**, §8.2.1, required — A.1 says this need not be documented, and it is documented here anyway because the environment variables are the user's only control over the defaults | Both defaults are decided **once per run unit, when the run unit starts** (`LocaleState`, created with the `RunUnit`), and they are always two separate settings. **User default locale:** the environment variable **`COBOL_USER_LOCALE`** if it is set; otherwise the process's current .NET culture (`CultureInfo.CurrentCulture`, which on Unix follows `LANG`/`LC_ALL`); otherwise the root locale. **System default locale:** the environment variable **`COBOL_SYSTEM_LOCALE`** if it is set; otherwise the host's installed UI culture (`CultureInfo.InstalledUICulture`); otherwise the root locale. The variables' values are locale names in the item-119 notation, trimmed; `C`, `POSIX`, `root` or `INVARIANT` names the root locale. **At least one user default and one system default always exist**: in an environment with no locale data at all (.NET invariant globalization), both fall back to the root locale and the run unit starts normally. The current locale starts as the user default for every category (§14.6.6). A program can change the user default for the rest of the run unit with `SET LOCALE USER-DEFAULT TO …`, but it cannot change the system default (§8.2.1: the capability is not provided), so `LocaleState.SystemDefault` has no setter. Example: with `COBOL_USER_LOCALE=de-DE` and `COBOL_SYSTEM_LOCALE=fr-FR`, `FUNCTION LOCALE-DATE("20260924")` gives `24.09.2026` after `SET LOCALE LC_ALL TO USER-DEFAULT` and `24/09/2026` after `SET LOCALE LC_ALL TO SYSTEM-DEFAULT`. COBOL-2002 onward. | — |
| DOC-A.1-119 | **Locale specification — manner of implementation**, §8.2.1, required — A.1 says this need not be documented, and it is documented here anyway because it decides which locale names a program may use | **A locale is named by a BCP 47 / CLDR language tag**, and POSIX-style spellings are accepted and normalized: `es_ES.UTF-8` is `es-ES`, `de-DE@phonebook` is `de-DE` with the phonebook collation, and `C`, `POSIX`, `root`, `INVARIANT` and the empty name are the **root locale** (`LocaleIdentification.Normalize`). **A locale is available** when WiseOwl COBOL has CLDR collation data for it, a site tailoring names it, or .NET recognizes it as a culture (`LocaleIdentification.IsAvailable`). A name that is none of these, such as `xx-NOWHERE`, is not available (EC-LOCALE-MISSING when a program selects it with checking on). The run unit keeps a current locale for **each of the six categories**, LC_COLLATE, LC_CTYPE, LC_MESSAGES, LC_MONETARY, LC_NUMERIC and LC_TIME, each set independently by SET LOCALE (`LocaleState`). **LC_COLLATE never comes from the host**: it is WiseOwl COBOL's own collation table generated from Unicode CLDR release 48-2 / UCA 17.0.0 data, so a locale orders text the same way on every host. **LC_CTYPE, LC_MONETARY and LC_TIME** content comes from the nearest predefined .NET culture: the tag, then its parents (`sr-Latn-RS`, then `sr-Latn`, then `sr`) (`LocaleFacts`). When no ancestor is a .NET culture, or the process runs in .NET invariant-globalization mode, that content is incomplete: the invariant culture's values stand in, and an operation that needs the category sets EC-LOCALE-INVALID when checking is on. The implementor default when the environment supplies none is the root locale (item 118). COBOL-2002 onward. | `unit:LocaleStateTests.ExternalIdentification_IsNormalized_PerDeterminationL1`; `unit:LocaleStateTests.Availability_IsTheOneKnownLocaleRule`; `unit:LocaleStateTests.Set_SwitchesOnlyTheNamedCategories_AndTheyPersist` |
| DOC-A.1-120 | **Locale switch — whether a switch of the current locale by a non-COBOL runtime module is recognized by COBOL**, §8.2.1, conditionally required (documented if provided) | **No, for every category.** WiseOwl COBOL never activates a non-COBOL module (a CALL cannot reach one, item 19). The only non-COBOL code that can share a run unit's process is a .NET host that activates a COBOL program, together with anything that host runs. The run unit reads the process culture **once, when it starts**, to determine its user and system default locales (item 118). After that, every locale-dependent operation uses only the run unit's own per-category state (`LocaleState`), and only SET LOCALE changes it. A later change of `CultureInfo.CurrentCulture`, `CultureInfo.DefaultThreadCurrentCulture` or the process's `LANG` by non-COBOL code is not utilized for LC_COLLATE, LC_CTYPE, LC_MESSAGES, LC_MONETARY, LC_NUMERIC or LC_TIME. Operations that are not locale-dependent never consult the process culture: DISPLAY of a floating-point or numeric item, NUMVAL and UPPER-CASE give the same result under `LANG=de_DE.UTF-8` or `tr_TR.UTF-8` as under `LANG=C`. A host that wants a different locale sets `COBOL_USER_LOCALE` (or its culture) before the run unit starts. COBOL-2002 onward. | — |
| DOC-A.1-122 | **National literals — number of hexadecimal digits that map to a national character**, §8.3.3.5.3 SR5, required + documented | **FOUR.** Each group of four hexadecimal digits in an `NX"…"` (or `NX'…'`) literal is one national character: the UTF-16 code unit with that value, which is also one national character position. `NX"004100420043"` is the three characters `ABC`, `FUNCTION LENGTH(NX"00410042")` is 2, and `FUNCTION ORD(NX"00E9")` is 234 (é is U+00E9). A character outside the Basic Multilingual Plane is written as its two surrogate code units and occupies two positions: `LENGTH(NX"D83DDE00")` is 2. A literal whose digit count is not a multiple of four, such as `NX"414"` or `NX"004100"`, is rejected with **COBOLNET1635** (`CobolLiteral.HexGroupViolation`, asked of every literal token by `LiteralScreenPass` — a keyword-omitted intrinsic argument such as `LENGTH(NX"041")` and a concatenation operand included, kb/Work PB1393). NX literals exist from COBOL-2002 on; at COBOL-85 they are COBOLNET0900. | `conformance:2023/r03_hexadecimal_national_and_boolean_literals` |
| DOC-A.1-125 | **NULL — the value of NULL**, §8.4.3.7.4 GR1 (object references) + §8.4.3.10.4 GR1–GR3 (data, program and function addresses), required — A.1 says this need not be documented, and it is documented here anyway because a pointer's NULL is visible in its storage image | **Object reference:** NULL is the .NET null reference, which never refers to an object, so `O = NULL` is true exactly when O refers to no object. **Data-pointer, program-pointer and function-pointer:** NULL is the default value of each pointer type (`ManagedPointer.Null`, `ProgramPointer.Null`, a `FunctionPointer` with no name), and it is distinct from every address `SET … TO ADDRESS OF` can produce: no data item, program or function has that address. **Storage image:** a NULL pointer of every category is eight X"00" bytes, the zero address, as in GnuCOBOL, IBM and Micro Focus. Any real address has a nonzero image (item 216). Every pointer and object-reference item starts as NULL, and SET … TO NULL restores it. The message-tag NULL (GR4) does not arise, because USAGE MESSAGE-TAG belongs to the message control system, which is not provided (COBOLNET1943). COBOL-2002 onward. | `conformance:2002/pb557_pointer_class_initial_value_is_null`; `unit:PointerImageTests.Null_OfEveryCategory_IsTheZeroAddress`; `conformance:2023/pb452_function_address_runtime` |
| DOC-A.1-126 | **OBJECT-COMPUTER paragraph — the default object computer**, §12.3.6.4 GR3 and GR4, required + documented | **The default object computer is the .NET runtime the program is compiled for**: the `Microsoft.NETCore.App` 10.0 shared framework (`net10.0`, named in the `.runtimeconfig.json` written beside every compiled assembly), on whatever operating system and processor it runs. There is only ONE object computer, so the default is the same whether the paragraph is written without computer-name-1 (GR3), omitted altogether with no containing source unit supplying one (GR4), or written with any computer-name (item 127: the name is accepted and identifies nothing). A program therefore behaves identically in all three cases: the same native collating sequence and character classification, and the PROGRAM COLLATING SEQUENCE and CHARACTER CLASSIFICATION clauses have the same effect with or without a name. Same in every edition. At COBOL-85 an OBJECT-COMPUTER paragraph that carries a clause must also carry computer-name-1; a paragraph with no clauses may omit the name in every edition. | `conformance:2023/pb373_object_computer_name_inert` |
| DOC-A.1-130 | **OPEN statement with the OUTPUT phrase — positioning of the output file with regard to physical page boundaries**, §14.9.27.4 GR18, required + documented | Physical pages have meaning for a **sequential-organization** file (record sequential or LINE SEQUENTIAL) — the files WRITE ... ADVANCING and the LINAGE clause apply to; a relative or indexed file has no pages. **OPEN OUTPUT creates the host file empty and places the output medium at its first byte, which is the TOP OF THE FIRST PHYSICAL PAGE.** The OPEN itself writes nothing — no form feed, no blank lines, no margin: a file opened OUTPUT and closed with no WRITE is **0 bytes** (`SequentialConnector.OpenWriter`). **No LINAGE clause:** because the medium is already at a page top, the first `WRITE ... AFTER ADVANCING PAGE` (or BEFORE) still repositions to the NEXT physical page as §14.9.51.4 GR25 h) says, by writing a form feed (U+000C) — so a file whose first WRITE advances PAGE begins with a form feed (`SequentialConnector.Advance`). **LINAGE clause:** at the completion of the OPEN the device is on line 1 of the first logical page, and that logical page begins at the file's first line; LINAGE-COUNTER is 1 (§13.18.34.4 GR7). The logical page's TOP margin lines are written onto the medium immediately before the first record is presented — never by the OPEN, so a LINAGE file closed with no WRITE is also 0 bytes (`SequentialConnector.BeginLinagePage`, `EmitTopMarginIfPending`). Example: `FD F LINAGE 5 FOOTING 4 TOP 2 BOTTOM 1`, OPEN OUTPUT, one plain `WRITE` of `AAA` gives two empty lines, then `AAA` (line terminator per item 114). Same in every edition. | `conformance:2023/pb523_linage_margins_on_the_medium`; `conformance:85/pb523_linage_margins_on_the_medium_85` |
| DOC-A.1-132 | **MODULE-NAME function — which type of dynamic-length elementary item is the returned value**, §15.65.4 r1, conditionally required + documented | WiseOwl COBOL supports more than one type of dynamic-length elementary item — alphanumeric and national, each either with the implementor-defined internal structure (no dynamic-length-structure-name, §8.5.1.10.2) or with a PREFIXED / DELIMITED structure named by a DYNAMIC LENGTH STRUCTURE clause (determination D-DL3 in §3) — so r1 requires the choice to be stated. **MODULE-NAME returns an ALPHANUMERIC dynamic-length elementary item with the implementor-defined internal structure** — the same type as a `PIC X DYNAMIC LENGTH` item that names no dynamic-length-structure-name: a native .NET `System.String`, one character per character position, whose maximum size is the implementor maximum of item 62 (1,073,741,791 characters). It is never national and never a PREFIXED or DELIMITED structure, so no PREFIXED length-field bound (65535 / 32767) applies to it. The value is exactly the name, with no trailing spaces (item 133): `FUNCTION LENGTH(FUNCTION MODULE-NAME(CURRENT))` in program `MNPROG` is 6, ACTIVATING in a main program is one space (length 1). Implemented as the `MODULE-NAME` entry of `IntrinsicCatalog` (result type alphanumeric), evaluated by `CobolModule.Name`. COBOL 2023 only — the function does not exist in earlier editions (COBOLNET1502). | — |
| DOC-A.1-138 | **Parameterized classes and interfaces — manner and time of expansion**, §7.1 (with §12.3.8.4 GR5 / GR8), required + need not be documented (documented voluntarily) | **Time:** a parameterized class or interface is expanded at compile time, once per compilation group, AFTER all text manipulation — COPY, REPLACE and the compiler directives have finished, the source has been parsed, and expansion runs at the start of binding (`OoExpansion.Expand`, called from `BinderDriver`). So a REPLACE that produces a formal parameter's name inside the definition takes part in the substitution. **Manner:** each distinct expansion (`CLASS new-name EXPANDS definition USING actual …` or the interface form in a REPOSITORY paragraph) becomes an ORDINARY class or interface: the definition's own tokens re-parsed with every occurrence of each formal parameter replaced by its actual parameter (§12.3.8.4 GR5 / GR8) and the header renamed to the expansion's name. It is never a .NET generic type, and from binding onward an expansion cannot be told from a written class — its FACTORY and OBJECT data are its own (two expansions of one definition keep separate factory counters). The parameterized definition itself generates nothing. **Identity (§9.3.12):** specifiers that repeat one (expansion name, definition, actual parameters) triple, in one or several source elements, create ONE class; reusing an expansion name with a different definition or different actuals is **COBOLNET2240**. COBOL 2002 and later. | `conformance:2002/pb759_parameterized_class` |
| DOC-A.1-139 | **PERFORM statement — whether location information is available when the LOCATION phrase is not specified**, §14.9.28.4 GR14 (through §7.3.25.4 GR7), required + documented | **Not available.** An exception-checking PERFORM (Format 3, `PERFORM … WHEN exception-name …  END-PERFORM`) enables the exception conditions its WHEN phrases name over imperative-statement-1 through an implicit TURN directive (§14.9.28.4 GR14), and that implicit directive carries LOCATION exactly when the PERFORM writes `WITH LOCATION`. Without it WiseOwl COBOL saves no location information for a condition raised there — the same determination as the explicit `>>TURN` directive (item 204), so neither route yields location data the other withholds. Consequence inside the WHEN handler and after the PERFORM: `FUNCTION EXCEPTION-LOCATION` returns one alphanumeric space, `FUNCTION EXCEPTION-LOCATION-N` one national space and `FUNCTION EXCEPTION-STATEMENT` 63 spaces, while `FUNCTION EXCEPTION-STATUS` still names the condition. With `PERFORM WITH LOCATION` the full location is produced — e.g. `PL; MAIN-P; 12` and statement `RAISE`. ⛔ **An explicit TURN that already enables the name wins, in both directions:** §14.9.28.4 GR14 assumes the implicit TURN only "if checking … is not enabled for imperative-statement-1 by a TURN directive", so when a `>>TURN … CHECKING ON` in force before the PERFORM already enables a name its WHEN phrase lists, no implicit directive is assumed for that name and THAT directive's LOCATION setting governs — under `>>TURN EC-USER-X1 CHECKING ON WITH LOCATION` a PERFORM without LOCATION still yields `PL2; MAIN-P; 7`, and under `>>TURN EC-USER-X1 CHECKING ON` (no LOCATION) a `PERFORM WITH LOCATION` yields one space. The LOCATION flag travels on `BoundExceptionPerform.WithLocation` into `TurnState.WithImplicitEnable`. COBOL 2023 only (Format 3 PERFORM is COBOLNET0900 in earlier editions). | `conformance:2023/l1_exception_no_location_arms`; `conformance:2023/exception_statement_ambient` |
| DOC-A.1-141 | **Procedure division header rules when the activating or the activated runtime element is not COBOL — restrictions and mechanisms (parameter matching, data type representation, returning of a value, omission of parameters)**, §14.2.3 GR13, conditionally required + documented | **Supported non-COBOL language products: any .NET language, and only as the ACTIVATING element.** No non-COBOL element can be ACTIVATED: there is no CALL-CONVENTION directive (item 19), and a CALL, a program-pointer or `ADDRESS OF PROGRAM` resolves only WiseOwl COBOL programs (otherwise EC-PROGRAM-NOT-FOUND). **Mechanism:** every generated program class implements `ICobolProgram`, and a .NET host activates the program as a called program through `ICobolProgram.Call(CobolArg[] args, CobolArg? returning)`. **Parameter matching:** positional, `args[0]` to the first USING formal and so on (§14.2.3 GR2) — never by name. A trailing formal with no argument is OMITTED; an argument beyond the last formal is ignored; this entry performs no argument-count check (EC-PROGRAM-ARG-MISMATCH for a count is raised only for a COBOL CALL, in `ProgramTable.CallProgram`). **Data type representation:** each argument is a `CobolArg(Mode, Carrier, Num, Layout, Length)`. `Carrier` is a `ManagedPointer<T>` over the host's own storage: a `ManagedPointer<string>` holding the item's character image (one UTF-16 character per character position) for an alphanumeric, national, edited or group formal, or a native numeric cell (`long`, `Int128`, `double`, …) whose representation `Num` (a `NumProfile`: digits, scale, sign and usage) describes; `Layout` describes a group that contains a table; `Length` is a text-carried item's fixed character length (`CobolArg.Unstated`, -1, when it has none), which a host states for the RETURNING item so that the delivery fits the result to it. A pointer or object-reference formal takes a `ManagedPointer<T>` of its own carrier type (`ManagedPointer`, `ProgramPointer`, `FunctionPointer` or the object's class), and `PredefinedNullArgument.Instance` passes the predefined NULL to any such formal. A formal of another shape sees the carrier through a converting view of the SAME storage (§14.2.3 GR8), so the program's stores reach the host's storage. `Mode` makes no copy: a COBOL CALL builds the BY CONTENT copy on the activating side, so a host that wants BY CONTENT passes a carrier over a copy it owns; a BY VALUE formal always adopts a detached copy (§14.2.3 GR10). A carrier the formal cannot read fails the activation with the .NET exception `CobolCallException` whose `EcName` is EC-PROGRAM-ARG-MISMATCH, before any statement of the program runs (`CobolArgAdapt`). **Omission of parameters:** pass `ManagedPointer.Null` as the carrier, or pass fewer arguments; the omitted-argument condition is then true (§14.9.4.4 GR11), a reference raises EC-PROGRAM-ARG-OMITTED when checking for it is enabled in the program (GR12), and otherwise reads an empty value (zero for a numeric formal). **Returning of a value:** the RETURNING item's content is delivered into the `returning` argument's carrier, described by that `CobolArg` exactly as a COBOL receiver is; GOBACK / EXIT PROGRAM is an ordinary method return to the host (§3, "Return to a NON-COBOL activating runtime element"). Same in every edition. | — |
| DOC-A.1-143 | **Program-name — formation rules for a non-COBOL program**, §8.3.2.2.21, optional (documented if provided) | **Not provided.** WiseOwl COBOL gives no program-name a non-COBOL meaning: no call-convention-name is defined (only `>>CALL-CONVENTION COBOL` is accepted, item 68) and there is no way to declare a non-COBOL program (item 19), so every program-name — in a CALL, a CANCEL, a program-pointer SET or `ADDRESS OF PROGRAM` — is formed and matched by the COBOL rules of §8.3.2.2 and resolves only to a WiseOwl COBOL program. A name that happens to spell a non-COBOL module (e.g. `CALL "System.Console"`) is simply a program that is not found: EC-PROGRAM-NOT-FOUND, the CALL's ON EXCEPTION phrase, and a NULL program-pointer from `ADDRESS OF PROGRAM` (`ProgramTable.ProbeSiblingModule` accepts only a module that registers WiseOwl COBOL programs). | `conformance:2002/w59h_non_cobol_names_not_found` |
| DOC-A.1-148 | **RECORD clause — whether format 3 (`RECORD CONTAINS integer-4 TO integer-5`) produces fixed-length or variable-length records**, §13.18.43.4 GR17, required + documented | **Variable-length.** A file whose RECORD clause is format 3 has variable-length records in the range integer-4 through integer-5 bytes: each WRITE or REWRITE writes the record named in the statement at that record's own size, and a READ delivers the size that was written — records are NEVER padded to integer-5. On the medium each record carries the framing item 151 describes: record sequential, relative and indexed files write a 4-byte little-endian length prefix before each record, LINE SEQUENTIAL files end each record with a line delimiter. Example: `FD F3 RECORD CONTAINS 5 TO 10 CHARACTERS` with `01 R3A PIC X(5)` and `01 R3B PIC X(10)`; writing R3A = `ABCDE` then R3B = `ABCDEFGHIJ` to a record sequential file gives the 23 bytes `05 00 00 00 'ABCDE' 0A 00 00 00 'ABCDEFGHIJ'`. Format 3 differs from format 2 (`RECORD IS VARYING …`) only where the standard ties a rule to the word VARYING — the READ INTO implicit move of §14.9.30.4 GR4 b) (`VaryingRecordInfo.VaryingClause`, set by `DataBinder.BindRecordClause`). Same in every edition. | `conformance:85/l1c23_record_format3_variable` |
| DOC-A.1-153 | **Record locking — the default mode when the user specifies none**, §12.4.5.9.4 GR1 b) 2., required + documented | **No record locking** — the third option GR1 b) 2. names. A file connector whose file control entry has neither a LOCK MODE clause nor a SHARING clause, opened by an OPEN with no SHARING phrase, SETS NO RECORD LOCKS: a READ does not lock, and an explicit `WITH LOCK` phrase on its I-O statements obtains nothing (`FileRegistry.ImplementorDefaultShare`, lock mode `FileLockMode.None`). It still RESPECTS other connectors' locks, because §9.1.16 makes a locked record inaccessible to every other file connector: its READ, REWRITE and DELETE of a record another connector has locked are refused with I-O status `51`, and READ … IGNORING LOCK reads it. Example (a connector with neither clause can be open beside another connector only in the input mode, because a clause-less OPEN I-O, EXTEND or OUTPUT establishes SHARING WITH NO OTHER, item 131): A is declared `SHARING WITH ALL OTHER` with `LOCK MODE IS MANUAL` and opens INPUT; B has neither clause and opens INPUT (`00`, `00`). A `READ … WITH LOCK` record 1 (`00`); B's READ of record 1 is `51`, B's `READ … IGNORING LOCK` of it is `00`, B's READ of the unlocked record 2 is `00`, and after A's UNLOCK B's READ of record 1 is `00`. A connector that must REWRITE or DELETE beside a locker declares `SHARING WITH ALL OTHER` and a LOCK MODE clause (§14.9.27.3 SR8) and then is refused a locked record the same way (`pb669_lock_visibility_plain_connector`: REWRITE and DELETE of a locked record are `51`). COBOL 2002 and later. | `conformance:2002/pb322_clauseless_reader_sees_record_lock`; `conformance:2002/pb669_lock_visibility_plain_connector` |
| DOC-A.1-154 | **Record locking — the maximum number of record locks a run unit may hold**, §12.4.5.9.4 GR7, required + documented | **255** — the standard's minimum (`PhysicalFileTable.RunUnitLockMax`). The count is every record lock held by every file connector of the run unit, over all physical files. An I-O statement that would obtain a 256th lock is unsuccessful with I-O status **`53`** (§9.1.13.8 item 3) and obtains no lock; a WRITE or REWRITE refused this way changes no record (the ceiling is checked before the record is touched, `PhysicalFileTable.PreflightNewLock`). Re-locking a record the same connector already holds does not count again. When the connector's own ceiling (item 155) is also reached, `54` is reported. COBOL 2002 and later. | `unit:CobolFileLockTests.RunUnitLockLimit_256thRunUnitLock_Is53` |
| DOC-A.1-155 | **Record locks — the maximum number of record locks one file connector may hold**, §12.4.5.9.4 GR7, required + documented | **15** — the standard's minimum (`PhysicalFileTable.ConnectorLockMax`). Only a connector with multiple record locking (`LOCK ON MULTIPLE RECORDS`) can hold more than one lock (GR6), and the MULTIPLE phrase is not allowed for sequential organization or access (§12.4.5.9.3 SR2), so the ceiling is reachable on relative and indexed files with random or dynamic access. An I-O statement that would obtain a 16th lock for the connector is unsuccessful with I-O status **`54`** (§9.1.13.8 item 4), obtains no lock and — for a READ — is an unsuccessful READ like any other (§14.9.30.4 GR18): no record position is established, so the next sequential READ is `46`, never the record after the refused one; a WRITE or REWRITE refused this way changes no record (`PhysicalFileTable.PreflightNewLock`). Re-locking a record the connector already holds does not count again; UNLOCK and CLOSE release its locks. COBOL 2002 and later. | `conformance:2023/pb338_read_lock_limit_position`; `unit:CobolFileLockTests.ConnectorLockLimit_16thRecord_Is54` |
| DOC-A.1-160 | **Report writer printable item — the fixed correspondence between columns and national characters**, §13.18.14.4 GR2, required + documented | **One column per national character position** — the same one-to-one correspondence as for an alphanumeric character. A national printable item described `PIC N(n)` (explicit or implied USAGE NATIONAL, including one inherited from a group entry) occupies exactly n columns, starting at the column its COLUMN clause names, and the horizontal counter advances by n (§13.18.14.4 GR9; `DataItem.DisplayTextWidth`, used by `ReportWriterEmitter.EmitFieldPlacements`). Example: `COLUMN 1 PIC N(4) SOURCE WN1` with WN1 = N"WXYZ" and `COLUMN 6 PIC X(3) VALUE "ABC"` on one line prints `WXYZ ABC` — the national item fills columns 1–4 and column 5 stays blank. COBOL 2002 and later (national data does not exist at `--std 85`: COBOLNET0900). Which bytes a national character becomes in the report FILE is item 159's record structure. | `conformance:2002/pb541_report_national_item` |
| DOC-A.1-169 | **SAME SORT AREA / SAME SORT-MERGE AREA clause — extent of allocation**, §12.4.6.4 GR4 b) (§12.4.6.4.4), required + need not be documented (VOLUNTARY) | **The extent is NONE.** A non-sort file named in a sort-merge-area format SAME clause never lends its storage to a SORT or MERGE: `DataBinder.BindIoControl` links only the SAME RECORD AREA format's STORAGE and gives the SORT AREA / SORT-MERGE AREA formats no storage effect (it records every format's MEMBERSHIP on its files, `FileModel.SameClauses`, which the SORT and MERGE statements read for §14.9.40.3 SR10 / §14.9.24.3 SR11 — a pair of the statement's own file-names in one barred clause is refused, kb/Work PB1139), and SORT/MERGE work storage is the run time's own managed memory, separate from every file's record area. GR4 b)'s "may be allocated" makes zero a permitted extent, and GR4 c) (the non-sort files do not share storage with each other) holds because the clause creates no sharing at all. The clause is accepted in every edition and a program that names a plain file in it sorts, reads and writes that file exactly as it would without the clause. | — |
| DOC-A.1-170 | **SEARCH ALL statement — varying of the search index during the search operation**, §14.9.37 GR9 (§14.9.37.4), required + need not be documented (VOLUNTARY) | **A forward scan in occurrence order.** The initial setting of the search index is ignored: SEARCH ALL sets it to occurrence **1**, evaluates the WHEN condition, and on a false result advances it by one, until a WHEN is satisfied or the last element has been tested (`ControlFlowEmitter.EmitAllScan`). The bound is the table's maximum occurrence count, the CURRENT count of an OCCURS DEPENDING ON table, or the current capacity of an OCCURS DYNAMIC table. The last-element test happens BEFORE the advance, so the index never holds a value outside 1 through the last element — also on an unsuccessful search, where the AT END phrase sees it at the last element tested; an empty table (current count 0) is unsuccessful with no element tested and the index at 1. On success the index holds the occurrence number of the element that satisfied the WHEN. No binary search is performed, so a table whose ASCENDING/DESCENDING KEY data is not actually in sequence still finds an element that satisfies the condition when one exists. GR9 makes the final setting after an unsuccessful search undefined, so a program shall not rely on which in-range value is left. | `conformance:85/pb447_search_all_index_stays_in_table` |
| DOC-A.1-181 | **SPECIAL-NAMES paragraph — allowable locale-names and literal values of the LOCALE clause**, §12.3.7 GR5 (§12.3.7.4), optional (documented if provided) | **Provided (2002 and later).** `LOCALE locale-name-1 IS {external-locale-name-1 or literal-4}` accepts the SAME set of identifications in both forms: a **locale tag** — any BCP-47 tag of a CLDR locale or of a culture .NET recognizes (`fr-FR`, `de-CH`, `sr-Latn-RS`, `zh-Hant`), a `-u-` extension allowed — or the root locale spelled `INVARIANT`, `ROOT`, `C`, `POSIX` or `und`. A **POSIX spelling** `ll_CC[.codeset][@modifier]` is normalized before use (`LocaleIdentification.Normalize`): underscores become hyphens, a `.codeset` suffix is ignored (the repertoire is always UTF-16), an `@modifier` that names a CLDR collation type becomes the `-u-co-<type>` extension (`de_DE@phonebook` is `de-DE-u-co-phonebook`), and any other modifier leaves a tag that names no locale. So `fr_FR`, `fr-FR`, `"fr_FR.UTF-8"` and `"fr-FR"` SELECT the same locale; the comparison is case-insensitive. Selecting the same locale is not having the same external identification: where a PICTURE clause identity is compared (the type-equivalence and parameter / returning-item conformance rules, which define the identification as "the external-locale-name or literal value" itself) the identification is compared as written, a literal value character for character (kb/Work PB1166). The word form is limited to what a COBOL word can spell (no `.` or `@`), so `.codeset` and `@modifier` spellings need the literal form. literal-4 shall be an alphanumeric or national literal (or a concatenation expression of that class), neither zero-length nor a symbolic-character figurative constant (§12.3.7.3 SR10/SR11, `COBOLNET0898`; every OTHER figurative constant is a literal of its class — §8.3.3.6.3 SR1, kb/Work PB1537 — so `LOCALE LA IS SPACE` names the locale `" "`, which is simply not available, and the predefined NULL is `COBOLNET2576`); a locale-name declared twice is `COBOLNET1665`. **Availability is decided at RUN time, never at compile time**: any spelling compiles, and a locale that is not available (`LocaleIdentification.IsAvailable` — the root, a locale with CLDR collation data, a site `.tailor` file, or a culture .NET recognizes) raises `EC-LOCALE-MISSING` at the statement that uses it when that check is enabled; with the check not enabled the statement proceeds with the root locale's conventions (a `LOCALE-DATE` through an unavailable `"zz_QQ"` or `"fr_FR@euro"` formats as `09/24/2026`, exactly as `INVARIANT` does). Before 2002 the clause is rejected (`COBOLNET0900`). | `unit:LocaleStateTests.ExternalIdentification_IsNormalized_PerDeterminationL1`; `unit:LocaleStateTests.Availability_IsTheOneKnownLocaleRule`; `conformance:2002/pb64t1_locale_declare`; `conformance:2002/pb1537_figurative_literal_clauses` |
| DOC-A.1-182 | **SPECIAL-NAMES paragraph, ALPHABET clause — coded character set referenced by the STANDARD-2 phrase**, §12.3.7 GR7 c) (§12.3.7.4), required + documented | **STANDARD-2 always references the ISO/IEC 646 International Reference Version — never a national version, under any circumstance** (no compiler option, locale or environment setting selects one). STANDARD-2 is therefore the same coded character set as STANDARD-1: its 128 characters are the first 128 positions of the native alphanumeric character set, U+0000 through U+007F, with the correspondence and ordinals item 187 states (`CodedCharacterSet.OrdinalCount` = 128; class membership `CobolClass.CodedSetKind.Ascii`). A class condition `IS alphabet-name` on a STANDARD-2 alphabet is true exactly for characters in U+0000–U+007F (`"AB~"` is, `é` is not), and a STANDARD-2 alphabet used as the PROGRAM COLLATING SEQUENCE collates in native order. The phrase is available for alphanumeric alphabets in every edition. | `conformance:85/l1c30_standard2_is_irv` |
| DOC-A.1-186 | **SPECIAL-NAMES paragraph, ALPHABET clause, literal phrase — ordinal number of the characters not specified**, §12.3.7 GR7 k) 4. (§12.3.7.4), required + documented (the scheme, not each character) | **The ordinal number of every character is its position in the alphabet's collating sequence, counting the first position as 1, and the unspecified characters are numbered in the order GR7 k) 3. collates them.** A literal-phrase alphabet's coded character set contains every native character — 65,536 UTF-16 code units, for an alphanumeric and a national alphabet alike (`CodedCharacterSet.OrdinalCount`). The characters the literal phrase specifies take ordinals 1 through *n*, one per collating position in the order written (a THROUGH range fills successive positions; an ALSO group shares one position, and that ordinal names ALL the group's characters — literal-1 first — per GR7 k) 6.). Which of them a reference uses depends on what it needs: a reference that needs ONE character takes literal-1 alone (`SYMBOLIC CHARACTERS … IN`, GR7 k) 6.'s last sentence: "only literal-1 is used to represent the character in the native character set"), a reference that names a SET takes them all (a numeric `CLASS … IN` literal, GR12 a, so `CLASS C1 IS 1 IN AL` over `ALPHABET AL IS "B" ALSO "A" ALSO "Z"` is {B, A, Z} — the singular "a character" of GR12 a is read as the plural because the other reading would make k) 6.'s exception redundant; kb/Work PB1095). A group's ordinal is no single character to begin or end a THROUGH run, so `CLASS C IS 1 THRU 3 IN AL` over such an ordinal is refused (COBOLNET1671, §12.3.7.4 GR12). The characters NOT specified then take ordinals *n*+1, *n*+2, … in ascending native code order, skipping the specified ones (`CodedCharacterSet.CharAt` for one character, `CodedCharacterSet.CharactersAt` for the set). Worked example: `ALPHABET AL IS "Z" "Y" ALSO "W" "X"` has *n* = 3, so ordinal 1 is `Z`, 2 is `Y`, 3 is `X`, 4 is U+0000, 5 is U+0001, and ordinal 70 is `B` (U+0042, the 67th unspecified character). These are the ordinals `SYMBOLIC CHARACTERS … IN alphabet-name` and a numeric `CLASS … IN alphabet-name` literal use. Because an ALSO character has no ordinal of its own, the highest ordinal is 65,536 minus the number of ALSO characters (in the example, ordinal 65,535 is U+FFFF and 65,536 is `COBOLNET1670`). The same in every edition. | `conformance:85/l1c30_literal_alphabet_unspecified_ordinals`; `conformance:2002/pb1095_class_in_also_group` |
| DOC-A.1-187 | **SPECIAL-NAMES paragraph, ALPHABET clause — correspondence of the STANDARD-1 and STANDARD-2 phrases with the native character set**, §12.3.7 GR7 c) (§12.3.7.4), required + documented | **The identity on U+0000–U+007F.** Both phrases reference ISO/IEC 646 IRV (item 182), and each of its 128 characters corresponds to the native alphanumeric character with the same code: ordinal *n* of the standard set is native character U+(*n*−1), so `SYMBOLIC CHARACTERS ... IN` a STANDARD-1/-2 alphabet accepts ordinals 1 through 128 (129 or more is `COBOLNET1670`), and ordinal 66 is `A`. The collating sequence is the native order of those characters. Native characters above U+007F have no counterpart in the standard set: they are not members for a class condition `IS alphabet-name` (`é` is not), and no ordinal reaches them. For `CODE-SET` the correspondence converts as the identity — a record of IRV characters is written to and read from the medium unchanged. **A record character above U+007F cannot be written under a STANDARD-1, STANDARD-2 or `ASCII` CODE-SET**: §13.18.13.4 GR6 b replaces each native character "with its associated coded character as defined in the alphabet being used", and such a character has none, so the WRITE or REWRITE is unsuccessful with the implementor-defined I-O status '91' (item 110) — '71' on a line sequential file, whose character set's ceiling is the file's coded character set (item 115) — nothing reaches the medium and the record area is unchanged. The `EBCDIC` code-set refuses a character above U+00FF the same way, and every organization decides it at the output statement, a relative or indexed store included although it reaches the medium only at CLOSE (kb/Work PB1150, PB1542). The one character every CODE-SET alphabet represents although no correspondence names it is HIGH-VALUE (U+FFFF): it is written as the coded character set's HIGHEST code unit (X'7F' under ISO/IEC 646, X'FF' under `EBCDIC`) and that unit reads back as HIGH-VALUE (owner decision kb/Work R51 item 4, 'cross a byte medium as that medium's extreme byte'; `CodeSetConversion.HighUnit`, kb/Work PB1759); the native character the alphabet assigns to that unit (DEL, U+009F) then reads back as HIGH-VALUE, the CODE-SET twin of item 31's `ÿ` cost. ⚠ **The input direction is a determination**: a medium byte X'80'–X'FF' is not a character of the set, and §13.18.13.4 names no condition for reading one, so a READ carries it into the record area as the native character of the same value under its ordinary status — '09' on a line sequential file, where it is outside the line sequential character set — and a relative or indexed store re-persisted at CLOSE writes it back byte-exactly; a WRITE or REWRITE of a record holding it is refused like any other non-member. Implemented by `CodedCharacterSet` (128 ordinals; its 128-entry identity correspondence is the file connector's `CodeSetConversion`, whose `Represents` is the membership `FileConnector.RecordHasCharacterWithoutByteImage` asks) and `CobolClass.CodedSetKind.Ascii`; the same in every edition. | `conformance:2023/pb109_class_condition_alphabet`; `conformance:2023/pb110_code_set_identity`; `conformance:85/pb1542_code_set_unrepresentable_85`; `conformance:2023/pb1542_code_set_line_sequential`; `unit:CodeSetConversionTests.Iso646_RepresentsExactlyItsOwn128Characters` |
| DOC-A.1-193 | **STOP and GOBACK statements — mechanism for error termination**, §14.9.42 GR4 (§14.9.42.4) · §14.9.18 GR9 (§14.9.18.4), optional (documented if provided) | **Not provided.** No implementor-defined mechanism can mark a run unit as terminating in error, so a `STOP RUN` or a main program's `GOBACK` written without an ERROR, NORMAL or STATUS phrase always reports a normal termination — process exit code **0** — whatever the program did before it. There is no `RETURN-CODE` special register (a reference to it is `COBOLNET1639`, not defined), no compiler option and no run-time setting that changes this; only the ERROR / STATUS phrases of the terminating statement (item 192) and an abnormal run-unit termination on a fatal exception condition (item 44), which is not a STOP or GOBACK, produce a nonzero exit code. | `conformance-test:StopGobackExitCodeTests.TerminationStatus_SetsProcessExitCode` |
| DOC-A.1-194 | **Subscripts — mapping of an index value to an occurrence number**, §8.4.2.3 GR1 c) (§8.4.2.3.4), required + need not be documented (VOLUNTARY) | **The identity.** An index holds its 1-based occurrence number directly — a 64-bit integer, never a byte offset or displacement (the storage of an index is item 211) — so subscripting by `index-name-1` selects the occurrence equal to the index value (`BoundIndexRef`; `NumericRenderer.Visit`). `index-name-1 + integer-1` and `index-name-1 − integer-1` select the occurrence the index value plus or minus the integer names, computed without changing the index. A `SET` of a numeric item to an index, or of an index to another index of the same table, carries that occurrence number unchanged. Example: with `05 E PIC X OCCURS 5 TIMES INDEXED BY I` holding "ABCDE", after `SET I TO 3` the references `E(I)`, `E(I + 1)` and `E(I - 2)` are `C`, `D` and `A`, and `SET N TO I` stores 3. | `conformance:85/pb215_index_windows` |
| DOC-A.1-196 | **SYNCHRONIZED clause — how the records of a file are handled**, §13.18.55 GR11 (§13.18.55.4), optional (documented if provided) | **Not provided.** WiseOwl COBOL has no rule for synchronizing the records of a file: a record area is never aligned or padded as a whole, and a SYNCHRONIZED elementary item in a record sits at exactly the offset it would have without the clause (item 195), so the record written to and read from the file is byte-for-byte the record's own image. Example: `01 R. 05 A PIC X. 05 B PIC S9(4) COMP SYNC. 05 C PIC X.` with "Q", 258 and "Z" writes the 4 bytes `51 01 02 5A` to a sequential file. The same in every edition. | `conformance:2023/l1_dns_a1_196_sync_file_record_stream` |
| DOC-A.1-197 | **SYNCHRONIZED clause — positioning when neither RIGHT nor LEFT is specified**, §13.18.55 GR3 (§13.18.55.4), required + documented | **The item is positioned exactly where it would be without the clause: it begins at the byte immediately after the preceding item.** WiseOwl COBOL treats every byte position as a natural boundary — a record is a typed .NET structure, not an addressable byte buffer, so an unaligned binary item costs no extra machine operations — and therefore performs no positioning, adds no slack bytes, and does not change the size of the item or of any containing group or REDEFINES (item 195). The same is true of `SYNCHRONIZED LEFT` and `SYNCHRONIZED RIGHT`: with a one-byte natural boundary an item both begins at the left byte and ends on the right byte of the boundaries that delimit it, so GR4 and GR5 are met with no adjustment, and the three forms lay out identically. Example: `01 G. 05 A PIC X. 05 B PIC S9(4) COMP SYNCHRONIZED. 05 C PIC X.` is 4 bytes, and `B` occupies bytes 2–3, with or without LEFT/RIGHT and with no clause at all. The same in every edition. | `conformance:2002/l1_byte_length_implicit_filler` |
| DOC-A.1-198 | **System-names — rules for the formation of a system-name**, §8.3.2.3 (§8.3.2.3.1), optional (documented if provided) | **Provided — one restriction.** A system-name is formed as any COBOL word of the target edition — letters, digits, hyphen, and (2002 and later) underscore, not beginning or ending with a hyphen or underscore (`ABC-` is a syntax error), at most 30 characters (COBOL-85), 31 (2002 and 2014) or 63 (2023), the length limit being `COBOLNET1567` — with ONE added restriction: a system-name shall not consist of digits only, because such a character-string is always read as an integer literal (`OBJECT-COMPUTER. 12345.` is a syntax error, `COBOL0001`). Unlike a user-defined word, a system-name need not contain a letter otherwise: `123ABC`, `MY_BOX-9` and `1-2` are all accepted as computer-names. Which system-names are RECOGNIZED is a separate matter: device-names, switch-names and feature-names are a fixed list (items 189–191; an unknown one is `COBOLNET2241`), code-names are items 183–184, and external-locale-names are item 181. | `conformance:85/l1c32_system_name_formation`; `conformance:negative/l1c32-system-name-all-digits` |
| DOC-A.1-201 | **Text manipulation — stage of processing at which parameterized classes and interfaces are expanded**, §7.2 (the sentence is in §7.1), required + need not be documented (VOLUNTARY) | **At compile time, after all text manipulation.** WiseOwl COBOL expands a parameterized class or interface when it BINDS the compilation group (`BinderDriver` → `OoExpansion`): for each `EXPANDS` specifier in a REPOSITORY paragraph it takes the parameterized definition's source text AS IT STANDS AFTER COPY, REPLACE and conditional compilation have been applied, substitutes each actual parameter for the corresponding formal parameter, and compiles the result as an ordinary, non-parameterized class or interface. So text manipulation can build or rename a formal parameter (a `REPLACE` that turns `ZZELEM` into `ELEM` before `CLASS-ID. ... USING ZZELEM` is seen gives the same class as writing `USING ELEM`), but a REPLACE never sees, and cannot alter, the text an expansion produces. Nothing is expanded at run time and no .NET generic type is used. Parameterized classes and interfaces exist from 2002 on; a class definition is rejected below 2002 (`COBOLNET0900`). | `conformance:2002/pb759_parameterized_class` |
| DOC-A.1-203 | **THROUGH phrase in the VALUE clause and the EVALUATE statement — collating sequence for a range when no alphabet-name is specified**, §14.7.8 rule 2, required + documented | **The PROGRAM COLLATING SEQUENCE of the range's class — the same sequence a relation condition between the same operands uses.** A range of alphanumeric (or alphabetic) values, in a level-88 `VALUE … THROUGH …` or an `EVALUATE … WHEN … THROUGH …`, written without `IN alphabet-name`, is evaluated in the alphanumeric program collating sequence declared by `OBJECT-COMPUTER. … PROGRAM COLLATING SEQUENCE` (§12.3.6), or in the native order (UTF-16 code-unit order) when none is declared; a range of national values uses the national program collating sequence (`… FOR NATIONAL`, 2002 and later), else the native national order (`CollatingSelection.ThroughRangeClass` → `CollatingSelection.ForComparison`). A national or alphanumeric GROUP conditional variable takes the sequence of its own class. A range whose first value collates after its second in that sequence is empty (with EC-RANGE-INVALID when that check is enabled). Example: with `PROGRAM COLLATING SEQUENCE IS EB` where `ALPHABET EB IS EBCDIC`, the value `"_"` is NOT in `"Z" THRU "a"` (in EBCDIC `a` collates before `Z`), while with no PROGRAM COLLATING SEQUENCE it is. Numeric ranges are algebraic (rule 1) and use no sequence. | `conformance:2002/pb398_range_in_alphabet`; `conformance:2002/pb728_level88_group_usage_collating` |
| DOC-A.1-213 | **USAGE NATIONAL — size and representation of characters**, §13.18.60 GR8 (§13.18.60.4), required + documented | **Two bytes per national character position, uniform at every length: one UTF-16 code unit, stored MOST SIGNIFICANT BYTE FIRST (UTF-16BE).** `PIC N(2)` occupies 4 bytes (`FUNCTION BYTE-LENGTH` 4, `FUNCTION LENGTH` 2), and a national-form numeric or numeric-edited item takes 2 bytes per symbol (`PIC 9(3) USAGE NATIONAL` is 6 bytes). The value of each position is the character's UTF-16 code unit — `A` is `00 41`, `é` is `00 E9`, `€` is `20 AC` — and this is the image seen through a REDEFINES, in an alphanumeric group that contains the item, and in a record written to a file. A character outside the Basic Multilingual Plane is a surrogate pair and occupies TWO character positions, each half treated as a separate character exactly as §8.5.1.4 rule 2 requires, so a MOVE that truncates between the halves keeps only the first. There is no option to vary the size or representation per compilation unit. USAGE NATIONAL exists from 2002 on (`COBOLNET0900` below). | `conformance:2023/pb646_national_form_numeric_bytes`; `conformance:2023/l1_usage_display_character_size` |
| DOC-A.1-220 | **Variable-length data items — actual time when the resources used are freed**, §8.5.1.11.3, required + need not be documented (VOLUNTARY) | **Whenever the .NET garbage collector reclaims them — a time the program cannot observe and WiseOwl COBOL does not fix.** No variable-length item has storage of its own to release: a DYNAMIC LENGTH elementary item is a managed .NET string, so the old value becomes unreachable when a new value is stored (a MOVE of "XY" over "ABCDEFGHIJ" leaves an item of length 2), and the collector reclaims it later. A dynamic-capacity table (`OCCURS DYNAMIC`, `CobolDynTable`) holds its occurrences in a managed array that grows by doubling and is NEVER shrunk: lowering the capacity (`SET … TO`, `DOWN BY`) only lowers the current count, keeping the removed occurrences' storage until the table is enlarged again — when each re-created occurrence is created afresh as a new occurrence, never with the value it held before — or until the table itself becomes unreachable. Freeing never affects a program's results. DYNAMIC LENGTH items and dynamic-capacity tables exist from 2014 on (`COBOLNET0900` below). | — |
| DOC-A.1-13 | **CALL statement — rules for program-name formation for a non-COBOL program**, §14.9.4.4 GR3 b), conditionally required (on support for calling non-COBOL programs) + documented | **Condition absent.** WiseOwl COBOL supports calling no non-COBOL program: the CALL-CONVENTION directive is recognized but every name is processed as a COBOL name (`DirectiveStateRegistry`, item 19), and there is no way to declare a program written in another language, so §14.9.4.4 GR3 b)'s implementor-defined branch ("If the program being called is not a COBOL program, the rules for program-name formation and for locating the program are defined by the implementor.") never applies. Every program-name is formed by the COBOL rules of §8.3.2.2 (item 143). A name that spells a non-COBOL module — even the WiseOwl COBOL runtime assembly `Cobol.Net.Runtime.dll`, which is deployed beside every program — is a program that cannot be located: EC-PROGRAM-NOT-FOUND, and the CALL's ON EXCEPTION phrase runs (`ProgramTable.ProbeSiblingModule` loads a sibling module only when it carries the WiseOwl COBOL `__CobolModule.Register` surface). Contingent: re-adjudicate if non-COBOL calls are ever supported. | `conformance:2002/w59h_non_cobol_names_not_found` |
| DOC-A.1-15 | **CALL statement — rules for locating a non-COBOL program**, §14.9.4.4 GR3 b), conditionally required (on support for calling non-COBOL programs) + documented | **Condition absent.** No non-COBOL program can be called (item 13), so there are no rules for locating one: the runtime locates only WiseOwl COBOL programs — those registered in the run unit, then a sibling module `<name>.dll` in the application directory that carries the WiseOwl COBOL registrar (`ProgramTable.ProbeSiblingModule`). A module without it, however it is named, is never loaded as a program and the CALL is not successful (EC-PROGRAM-NOT-FOUND, §14.9.4.4 GR3 b)). Contingent: re-adjudicate if non-COBOL calls are ever supported. | `conformance:2002/w59h_non_cobol_names_not_found` |
| DOC-A.1-30 | **Compiler directives — the IMP directive (syntax rules and general rules)**, §7.3.3 SR9 + §7.3.4 GR4, conditionally required (on support for an IMP directive) + documented if provided | **Condition absent.** WiseOwl COBOL defines no IMP directive. §7.3.3 SR9 reserves the word ("The compiler-directive word 'IMP' is reserved for use by the implementor.") and §7.3.4 GR4 makes its rules implementor-defined only "If the implementor defines the IMP directive". The recognized directives are exactly the rows of `CompilerDirectiveCatalog`, which has no IMP row, so a `>>IMP` line is left in the text as an unrecognized directive and the compilation is refused at every edition (a syntax error at the `>>`, COBOL0001). Contingent: re-adjudicate if an IMP directive is ever defined. | `conformance:negative/w59h-imp-directive-undefined` |
| DOC-A.1-34 | **Computer's coded character set — for literals, the correspondence between the compile-time and runtime character sets when conversion takes place**, §8.1.2, conditionally required (on the two sets differing) + documented if provided | **Condition absent: the two sets are the same, so no conversion takes place.** §8.1.2 permits them to differ ("The coded character set used during compilation may be the same as or different from the coded character set used during execution of the resultant runtime elements."); in WiseOwl COBOL both are the UTF-16 repertoire (item 33). The source text is decoded to UTF-16 when it is read (`CompilationInputs.ReadAllText`), a literal's characters are stored as those code units, and the run time represents every character by the same code unit — so a literal means at run time exactly what it meant at compile time: `FUNCTION ORD("€")` is 8365 (U+20AC plus 1) and `FUNCTION CHAR(8365)` equals the literal `"€"`. | `conformance:2002/l1c01_native_ordinal_positions_wide` |
| DOC-A.1-37 | **Computer's coded character set — when more than one encoding is supported, the mechanism for selecting the encoding for runtime**, §8.1.2, conditionally required (on more than one encoding being supported) + documented if provided | **Condition absent: exactly one encoding is supported.** The computer's alphanumeric and national coded character sets are both encoded as UTF-16 code units, one per character position (item 33); no directive, compiler option, environment variable or runtime call selects another, so there is no selection mechanism to document. (The CODE-SET clause and the ALPHABET clause's code-names convert at the FILE boundary; they do not change the computer's coded character set.) An ordinal therefore depends only on the character, never on the host: `FUNCTION ORD("€")` is 8365 on every machine. Contingent: re-adjudicate if a second encoding is ever supported. | `conformance:2002/l1c01_native_ordinal_positions_wide` |
| DOC-A.1-88 | **Function-identifier — execution of a non-COBOL function when a function-prototype-name is specified**, §8.4.3.2.4 GR6 d), conditionally required (on support for calling non-COBOL functions) + documented | **Condition absent.** WiseOwl COBOL supports calling no non-COBOL function, so §8.4.3.2.4 GR6 d)'s implementor-defined branch ("the function to be activated is not a COBOL function, the execution is defined by the implementor") never applies. A function-prototype-name locates only a WiseOwl COBOL user-defined function registered in the run unit (`ProgramTable.CallProgram`, function arm); a prototype whose externalized name spells a non-COBOL module is a function that is not found — EC-FUNCTION-NOT-FOUND (§8.4.3.2.4 GR6 b)), fatal unless checking is enabled and a declarative takes it. Contingent: re-adjudicate if non-COBOL functions are ever supported. | `conformance:2002/w59h_non_cobol_names_not_found` |
| DOC-A.1-96 | **Hexadecimal alphanumeric literals — mapping when characters are not multiples of four bits**, §8.3.3.2.4 GR5, conditionally required (on a coded character set whose characters are not multiples of four bits) + documented if provided | **Condition absent.** §8.3.3.2.4 GR5 defers "the mapping of each hex-character-sequence-1 to a character, when the characters do not occupy a multiple of four bits"; in WiseOwl COBOL an alphanumeric character is taken from exactly TWO hexadecimal digits (8 bits, item 9 / §8.3.3.2.3 SR6) and occupies a 16-bit code unit — both multiples of four — so the case cannot arise. A hex-character-sequence of any other digit count is refused (COBOLNET1635), and every two-digit group maps to the character U+00hh (item 95). | `conformance:2002/l1c12_hex_alphanumeric_every_group`; `conformance:negative/r03-hex-literal-digit-grouping` |
| DOC-A.1-98 | **Hexadecimal national literals — mapping when characters are not multiples of four bits**, §8.3.3.5.4 GR5, conditionally required (on a coded character set whose characters are not multiples of four bits) + documented if provided | **Condition absent.** A national character is taken from exactly FOUR hexadecimal digits (§8.3.3.5.3 SR5) and is one 16-bit UTF-16 code unit, a multiple of four bits, so the mapping §8.3.3.5.4 GR5 defers for characters that are not cannot arise. Any other digit count is refused (COBOLNET1635); every group is stored verbatim as its code unit (item 97). | `conformance:2002/l1c12_hex_national_no_character`; `conformance:negative/r03-hex-literal-digit-grouping` |
