| DOC-A.1-142 | **Program-address-identifier — relation between the address and a non-COBOL program**, §8.4.3.13.4 GR2, conditionally required (on support of non-COBOL programs) + documented | **Condition absent.** §8.4.3.13.4 GR2 admits a program "written in COBOL or in another language for which the implementor has declared support"; WiseOwl COBOL declares support for no other language (item 13), so its implementor-defined sentence ("For a non-COBOL program, the relation between the address and the associated program is defined by the implementor.") never applies. `ADDRESS OF PROGRAM` locates only WiseOwl COBOL programs (`ProgramRegistry.EntryOfOperand`); a name that spells a non-COBOL module cannot be located, so the address is NULL (§8.4.3.13.4 GR4). Contingent: re-adjudicate if non-COBOL programs are ever supported. | `conformance:2002/w59h_non_cobol_names_not_found` |
| DOC-A.1-149 | **RECORD DELIMITER clause — consistency rules when used with external file connectors**, §12.4.5.3 GR1 c), conditionally required (on the optional feature-name-1 existing) + documented if provided | **Condition absent.** §12.4.5.3 GR1 c) asks for "a consistent value of feature-name-1 in the RECORD DELIMITER clause" and "The implementor shall specify the consistency rules for feature-name-1"; WiseOwl COBOL provides no feature-name-1 (item 150, 'Not provided.'), and the RECORD DELIMITER clause as a whole is declined with a named warning (COBOLNET1778, `DiagnosticCatalog.RecordDelimiterUnsupported`, bound in `DataBinder.BindFileControl`), so no value exists to be consistent. Contingent: re-adjudicate if a feature-name-1 is ever provided. | `conformance:2023/pb292_record_delimiter_witness`; `conformance-test:DocumentedNonSupportWitnessTests.RecordDelimiterFeatureName_NamedNonSupportWarning_PinnedToSpec` |
| DOC-A.1-152 | **Record locking — circumstances other than a locked logical record that return a locked record status**, §9.1.16, conditionally required (on such circumstances existing) + documented if provided | **Condition absent: there are none.** §9.1.16 permits them ("The implementor may specify circumstances other than a locked logical record that result in the return of a locked record status."); WiseOwl COBOL specifies none. I-O status `51` (§9.1.13.8 item 1) is produced in exactly two places, each only for a record another file connector holds locked: `PhysicalFileTable.LockRecord` (the record's lock owner is a different connector) and `FileRegistry.ConflictOnLockedRecord` (`PhysicalFileTable.IsLockedByOther`). A new producer turns the pinning drift test red, which is this row's re-adjudication trigger. | `unit:LockedRecordStatusProducersDriftTests.LockedRecordStatus_IsProducedOnlyWhereAnotherConnectorHoldsTheLock`; `conformance:2002/pb669_lock_visibility_plain_connector` |
| DOC-A.1-200 | **Text manipulation — stage of processing the LISTING and PAGE directives and the SUPPRESS phrase of COPY**, §7.3.18.1 + §7.3.19.1 + §7.2.3.4 GR4, conditional (on the implementor producing listings), need not be documented | **Condition absent.** The compiler never produces a source listing (item 117), so the stage at which these three listing controls are processed has no effect to document. `>>LISTING` is ignored (§7.3.18.3 GR1, "If the compiler does not produce a source listing, the LISTING directive shall be ignored"), `>>PAGE` has no effect (§7.3.19.4 GR3, "If a source listing is not being produced, a PAGE directive shall have no effect."), and COPY's SUPPRESS PRINTING phrase affects only the listing (§7.2.3.4 GR4): the library text is incorporated in full. All three are accepted and inert (`DirectiveStateRegistry`, NONE HELD; `CopyProcessor.ParseCopyStatement`, which parses SUPPRESS [PRINTING] in its general-format place, before the REPLACING phrase — kb/Work PB1354). Contingent: re-adjudicate if a listing is ever produced. | `conformance:2002/w59h_listing_stage_inert`; `conformance:2002/pb725_directive_facility` |
| DOC-A.1-14 | **CALL statement — runtime resources that are checked**, §14.9.4.4 GR3c, required + documented | **The set of runtime resources checked is EMPTY, so a CALL never raises EC-PROGRAM-RESOURCES.** WiseOwl COBOL checks no runtime resource beyond locating the program (§14.9.4.4 GR3b), and a failure to locate it is “the program cannot be located”: a callee is located when it is registered in the run unit's program table — compiled into the calling assembly, or a separately compiled module `<name>.dll` in the application directory whose `__CobolModule.Register()` registrar has run (`ProgramTable.ProbeSiblingModule`; the rest of the locating mechanics are implementor-defined, see items 15 and 16). A module file that exists but cannot be loaded (a truncated or foreign image, or a dependency the registrar itself needs) or whose registrar throws has therefore NOT been located, and the CALL takes GR3b's arm: **EC-PROGRAM-NOT-FOUND**, the call is not successful, and execution continues per GR3h (ON EXCEPTION, or the checking-state disposition). Once a program is located no further resource is checked before control transfers (GR3g). This follows GnuCOBOL under CLAUDE.md rule 1's precedence — libcob's resolver sets EC-PROGRAM-NOT-FOUND when a module is found but cannot be loaded, and has no EC-PROGRAM-RESOURCES raise site on CALL — except that GnuCOBOL also prints a runtime warning naming the module's path, which WiseOwl COBOL does not issue. The same determination serves a user-defined function (item 89) and the class of an invoked method (item 102). | `conformance:2023/l1_call_unloadable_module_not_found` |
| DOC-A.1-20 | **Case mapping (compile-time, of the COBOL character repertoire)**, §8.1.3 (§8.1.3.2 GR1 with GR3 b) and GR4 b)), required + documented | **WiseOwl COBOL's compile-time case mapping IS Annex C (C.2 General case mappings) of the edition being compiled, applied as a fold from uppercase to lowercase** — the Unicode simple lowercase mapping of the Annex B characters that the A.1 item 20 NOTE recommends (C.2's own NOTE: the Changes_When_Lowercased property, Unicode 13.0.0). **Basic letters:** A–Z fold to a–z everywhere outside the non-hexadecimal forms of alphanumeric and national literals (§8.1.3.2 GR3 a/b), so `WS-Total` and `ws-total` are one word. **Extended letters** (A.4.6, claimed in §5): an uppercase extended letter folds to the lowercase letter C.2 pairs it with, and a letter C.2 does not list folds to nothing but itself (GR4 b), so `CAFÉ` and `café` are one word while dotless `ı` (U+0131) is distinct from `i` at COBOL 2023. The per-edition differences follow the annex: COBOL 2023 deletes `(0131,0069)` and `(03C2,03C3)` (E.2 item 14) and adds the E.3.3 item 6 pairs, so a 2002/2014 compilation folds by the older list. **Implementation:** ONE fold, `CobolNames` (the C.2 table transcribed from this standard by `scripts/spec/gen_annex_bc.py`, `AnnexBCDriftTests`), keys every name table and every word comparison of the compiler, the frontend and the run-time externalized-name match (`ExternalizedNames`, INVOKE's method-name dispatch); .NET's ordinal-ignore-case comparison, which disagrees with C.2 on the extended letters, is barred there (`CobolNameFoldDriftTests`). The table is COBOL 2023's; below 2023 the lexer writes E.2 item 14's two letters (U+0131, U+03C2) in their edition's fold, which is the only difference a COBOL 2002/2014 word can observe — every mapping E.3.3 item 6 added pairs a character those editions' Annex B lacks (kb/Work PB1402). This compile-time mapping governs COBOL WORDS only; the RUNTIME mapping of the UPPER-CASE / LOWER-CASE functions is the separate §3 determination (the .NET invariant tables, or a locale's LC_CTYPE), and item 43's currency-symbol equivalence is its own rule. | `unit:CobolNameFoldDriftTests.Same_IsTheAnnexCFold`; `unit:AnnexBCDriftTests.AnnexCLists_EqualTheSpec`; `conformance:2023/pb1402_extended_letters_2023` |
| DOC-A.1-23 | **Characters prohibited from use in text-words in COPY … REPLACING and REPLACE**, §7.2.2.5 rule 3, required + documented | **NONE — WiseOwl COBOL prohibits no character in, or as, a text-word.** Any character of the compile-time coded character set (UTF-16, item 25) that is not a separator is an ordinary text-word character, whether or not it is in the COBOL character repertoire: `==éVAL==`, `==@Y~==` and `==€X==` are pseudo-text operands like any other and match exactly the text-words they spell, in both COPY … REPLACING and REPLACE, at every edition. What bounds a text-word is the §8.3.5 separator set (with the §7.2.2.5 rule 1 colon and parenthesis cases), and the end of a source line — and nothing else (a horizontal tab U+0009 never reaches the text-word stage: the line-entry stage expands every tab to spaces before anything reads the line, as GnuCOBOL's reader does, item 157) — so a character that merely LOOKS like a space but is not the COBOL character space — U+00A0 NO-BREAK SPACE, U+2000–U+200A, U+3000 IDEOGRAPHIC SPACE, U+0085 — is a text-word character, and `==P<U+00A0>Q==` is ONE text-word that matches `P<U+00A0>Q` and never the two words `P Q`. Whether the text that results from replacement is a legal COBOL word is decided afterwards by the ordinary lexical rules, not by the text-manipulation pass. The separator-space set is ONE predicate, `TextWordScanner.IsSeparatorSpace` (space and line end — the lexer's `WS` set), used by every text-word boundary the text-manipulation stage draws (kb/Work PB1543). | `conformance:85/pb1543_nbsp_is_a_text_word_character`; `unit:TextManipulationTextWordTests.UnicodeSpaceLookalike_IsATextWordCharacter` |
| DOC-A.1-25 | **COBOL character repertoire — encoding of, mapping of, substitute graphics**, §8.1.3.2 GR1 and GR5, required + documented | **Mapping:** every basic letter, basic digit, basic special character and extended letter maps to its ISO/IEC 10646 code point, held as UTF-16 — one coded character set serves both the alphanumeric and the national class, so the two share one numbering and GR1's alphanumeric-to-national correspondence is the identity (`A` is U+0041 in both; the figurative and editing values are item 32's row). **Encoding of source text:** decided per file (item 26's row): a byte-order mark selects UTF-8, UTF-16LE/BE or UTF-32, and a file with no mark is read as UTF-8. **A file with no mark that is not well-formed UTF-8 is read as ISO/IEC 8859-1 instead** — every byte is the character with that code point, so no source byte is lost or altered (a 0xE9 byte in `VALUE "A<0xE9>B"` is `é`, U+00E9, `FUNCTION ORD` 234) — and the compiler issues a WARNING naming the file, so the choice is never silent. **A file whose byte-order mark names an encoding its content is not well-formed in** (invalid UTF-8 after `EF BB BF`, a lone surrogate in UTF-16, a UTF-32 unit beyond U+10FFFF) **is a compile-time ERROR naming the file and the byte offset** — there is no fallback for a marked file, because the mark states the encoding. No source character is ever replaced by U+FFFD or any other stand-in. This is the byte-preserving disposition GnuCOBOL takes under CLAUDE.md rule 1's precedence (cobc runs no conversion on source text, so each byte is one character with that byte's code). **Substitute graphics:** none; every character of the COBOL repertoire has its own graphic in ISO/IEC 10646, so GR5 never applies. The same at every edition. ⚠ **Not yet what the compiler does for malformed source text:** `CompilationInputs.ReadAllText` (`File.ReadAllText`) replaces each invalid byte sequence with U+FFFD and says nothing (`FUNCTION ORD` 65534), in literals, comments and library text alike, and whether or not the file has a byte-order mark (kb/Work PB1397); the mapping and BOM-selected encodings already behave as stated. | — |
| DOC-A.1-31 | **Computer's coded character set — the characters in and the encoding of the alphanumeric and national coded character sets, and the encoding for USAGE DISPLAY and USAGE NATIONAL**, §8.1.2 (with §8.3.3.2.3 SR2 and §8.3.3.5.3 SR2 for source representation), required + documented | **ONE coded character set serves both classes: the UTF-16 Basic Multilingual Plane, one code unit per character position** (the standing UTF-16 repertoire decision, applied in kb/Work R48's second section; items 33, 188). The alphanumeric and the national coded character set each contain all 65,536 BMP code units; a character outside the BMP is a surrogate pair occupying two character positions (§8.5.1.4, item 213). **In memory** every character is its UTF-16 code unit, whatever the item's class. **USAGE NATIONAL** is two bytes per character position, the code unit most significant byte first (UTF-16BE, item 213). **USAGE DISPLAY** is one byte per character position in ISO/IEC 8859-1 (item 209), under the **storage-byte law** (owner decisions kb/Work R51 / R52; kb/Work PB1759; design COBOLNET_FILES D29; `CobolNet.Runtime.StorageByte`): a character U+0000–U+00FE is the byte of the same value and the byte 0xFF is the HIGH-VALUE character U+FFFF (§8.3.3.6.4 GR6 — the highest of the 65,536 native characters), so the characters with a DISPLAY byte image are U+0000–U+00FF and U+FFFF, and **U+00FF and U+FFFF share the byte 0xFF**. The same law images and decodes every byte-form storage position (binary, packed, IEEE, pointer, bit, a national item's UTF-16BE pairs in a record image — a character's byte is its low-order byte), which is what keeps `MOVE HIGH-VALUES TO REC` followed by `IF REC = HIGH-VALUES` TRUE for a record with a COMP field (§14.9.25.4 GR4 moves a group "without conversion of data from one form of internal representation to another"). **Accepted cost (owner R52):** a real `ÿ` (U+00FF) that passes through a byte — the medium of a fixed-record file, or a byte-form storage position — returns as HIGH-VALUE; in an alphanumeric item in memory it stays U+00FF, and so does the literal `X"FF"`. **Any other character above U+00FF has no DISPLAY byte image, and WiseOwl COBOL never loses it silently.** At an in-memory byte channel (the storage image read by CONVERT's ANY, HEX and BYTE legs, a REDEFINES or group image) it becomes `?` (0x3F) and EC-DATA-CONVERSION is set (item 33's decided disposition). At a FIXED-RECORD file with no CODE-SET clause — record sequential, report, relative and indexed files (owner decision kb/Work R47) — the WRITE or REWRITE of a record holding one is UNSUCCESSFUL with the implementor's I-O status `'91'` (§9.1.13.11; item 110), nothing reaches the medium, and the record area is unchanged. A print line is no exception: it is written one byte per column in the same set, U+0080–U+00FF included, with no print-specific character map (item 159). `FileCharacterSet` is the fixed-record set's one executable copy (`ToChannel` / `FromChannel` apply the law at the medium), and every record write encodes through its STRICT ISO/IEC 8859-1 encoding, whose exception fallback makes a write that skipped the refusal fail loudly instead of writing `?`. Every byte read decodes by the law (0xFF as U+FFFF). **A LINE SEQUENTIAL file with no CODE-SET clause is UTF-8 TEXT** (owner decision kb/Work R51, superseding R47 for that organization; kb/Work PB1760; item 115; `LineSequentialEncoding`): every character a record area holds round-trips, U+00FF included, and only a character outside the line sequential character set is refused (`'71'`). A CODE-SET clause selects the medium encoding instead (§5 row 27), and the CODE-SET alphabet's coded character set then decides what can be written: a character it does not represent is refused with the same '91' / '71' (item 187; kb/Work PB1542). **Source representation:** a source file is read as UTF-8 unless a byte-order mark names UTF-16 or UTF-32 (ISO/IEC 8859-1 when it has no mark and is not well-formed UTF-8, item 25), and every source character is designated a character of both compile-time sets, so an alphanumeric or a national literal may contain any BMP character; the compile-time and runtime sets are the same (item 34), e.g. `FUNCTION ORD("€")` is 8365. Every edition (the national set from 2002). | `conformance:2002/l1c01_native_ordinal_positions_wide`; `conformance:2002/pb59_repertoire_identity`; `conformance:85/pb690_file_character_set_85`; `conformance:2023/pb690_line_sequential_byte_image_2023`; `unit:FileBoundaryTests.FileCharacterSet_IsLatin1_AndRefusesRatherThanReplaces` |
| DOC-A.1-36 | **Computer's coded character set — when alphanumeric and national are one composite set, the characters that map into each**, §8.1.2, conditionally required (condition met: one UTF-16 set serves both classes, item 31) + documented | **Every character maps into BOTH sets: the alphanumeric and the national coded character set are the same 65,536 UTF-16 BMP code units** (the standing UTF-16 repertoire decision, applied in kb/Work R48's second section; item 31). No character is national-only and none is alphanumeric-only, so a PIC X item may hold every character a PIC N item may, a MOVE between the classes is the identity (item 33), and no character in an alphanumeric item is ever mixed alphanumeric and national data (item 38). Which characters have a ONE-BYTE USAGE DISPLAY image (U+0000–U+00FF) is the encoding question, answered by item 31, not a mapping: a character above U+00FF is still an alphanumeric character, and only its byte image is refused or substituted there. Every edition from 2002 (the national set). | `conformance:2002/pb59_repertoire_identity`; `conformance:2002/l1c01_native_ordinal_positions_wide` |
| DOC-A.1-38 | **Computer's coded character set — whether UTF-8 or mixed alphanumeric and national characters are recognized in class alphanumeric, and the applicable syntax and general rules**, §8.1.2; §8.3.3.2.3 SR2; §8.3.3.2.4 GR3 c), conditionally required (on providing the capability) + documented if provided | **Not provided — and no character needs it.** Every UTF-16 BMP character is a character of the alphanumeric coded character set (items 31, 36), so a format 1 alphanumeric literal or an alphanumeric data item containing `€` or `Ж` holds only alphanumeric characters, one per character position; mixed alphanumeric and national data cannot arise. UTF-8 is not recognized in class alphanumeric: a UTF-8 source file is decoded before lexing, so `"€é"` is two characters (`FUNCTION LENGTH` 2), never the three or more bytes of its UTF-8 form, and no option keeps the bytes. There are therefore no additional syntax rules (SR2) or general rules (GR3 c) to specify. Every edition from 2002. | `conformance:2002/l1c01_native_ordinal_positions_wide` |
| DOC-A.1-39 | **CONTINUE statement — precision and maximum size of the AFTER interval**, §14.9.9.4 GR1, required + documented | **m = 0, n = 5, and the maximum meaningful value is 86,400 seconds (one day)** (`CobolTiming.MaxSeconds`). The interval behaves as a temporary `9(5)` item with no decimal places: a value of arithmetic-expression-1 **greater than 86,400 — however large, in any arithmetic mode and whatever the expression's form (literal, data item, product, quotient, power) — puts 86,400 in the temporary**, so the program is suspended for one day and never for less; any other value is stored by the implicit COMPUTE without ROUNDED, so a fraction truncates toward zero (`CONTINUE AFTER 1.9 SECONDS` suspends 1 second, `0.999…` suspends none). A value below zero is set to 0 and, with FC-CONTINUE-LESS-THAN-ZERO checking enabled, raises the nonfatal EC-CONTINUE-LESS-THAN-ZERO (GR1 a–c). Because m = 0, the processor-dependent fractional precision of Annex A.3 item 8 is not claimed (§2 row 8). COBOL 2023 only (CONTINUE AFTER is new in 2023). The interval is evaluated once and handed to the runtime on the carrier it evaluated on (the scaled `Int128`, the unsigned-wide `UInt128`, the standard-decimal `CobolDec` or binary64), and `CobolTiming.ContinueAfter` takes its sign and its whole seconds from that value in its own domain through the carrier's saturating reader (`CobolNum.PositionOf`), so a value past every host integer — 2^64, 2^64 + 3, 10^40, a 16-byte unsigned `COMP-5` item at or above 2^127 — is above the maximum and suspends exactly 86,400 seconds (kb/Work PB1529; witnesses are observed through `CobolTiming.SuspensionObserver`, never timed). | `unit:CobolTimingTests.HugeInterval_SuspendsForTheMaximumMeaningfulValue`; `unit:CobolTimingTests.ScaledLane_HugeInterval_SuspendsForTheMaximum`; `unit:CobolTimingTests.UnsignedWideLane_HugeInterval_SuspendsForTheMaximum`; `unit:CobolTimingTests.SdidiLane_HugeInterval_SuspendsForTheMaximum`; `unit:CobolTimingTests.ContinueAfterCall_IsTotalOverTheNumXCarriers_AndNarrowsNothing`; `unit:CobolTimingTests.CompiledProgram_EveryLaneSuspendsForTheMaximum_NeverForTheWrappedValue`; `unit:CobolTimingTests.CompiledProgram_StandardDecimal_EveryExpressionSuspendsForTheMaximum` |
| DOC-A.1-40 | **COPY statement — rules for identifying and locating default library text**, §7.2.3.3 SR5 and §7.2.3.4 GR3 (with GR2), required + documented | **The default COBOL library is an ordered list of places, searched in GnuCOBOL's order** (CLAUDE.md rule 1: the standard leaves both the list and the search to the implementor): (1) the compiler process's current working directory — the name is first tried as a path relative to it, or as written when it is absolute; (2) each `--copy DIR` in command-line order; (3) with `--nist`, the `copylib` directory beside the source file's directory. The source file's own directory is searched only when it is one of these — as in GnuCOBOL 3.2, whose own testsuite carries "COPY: relative copybooks" (a COPY resolved against the including source's directory) as an expected failure, not implemented. The same list serves every COPY in the compilation group, including a COPY inside library text. **Locating a text:** text-name-1 is used as spelled; literal-1's content is a host path (SR5: any non-empty host path is an allowable literal-1 or literal-2) — its VALUE, so a doubled quotation symbol is one character and a hexadecimal-format `X"…"` names the characters its digit pairs encode; a national or boolean literal, a concatenation expression or a figurative constant is refused as COBOLNET2450 (SR4/SR5; kb/Work PB1354). In each place in turn WiseOwl COBOL tries the name as spelled and then with the suffixes `.CPY`, `.CBL`, `.COB`, `.cpy`, `.cbl`, `.cob`, in that order (a name whose FILE NAME — the part after the last directory separator — already contains a period is tried only as spelled; `"../lib/BOOK"` still takes the suffixes), and the FIRST regular file that exists IS the library text; so a text-name names exactly one text in a library (SR3) and no ambiguity is diagnosed. If `BK3.cbl` and `BK3.cob` sit in one directory, `COPY BK3` is `BK3.cbl`. Whether letter case in the name matters is the host file system's rule. **OF/IN library-name-1 or literal-2** names a subdirectory: the FIRST place (in the same order) that holds a subdirectory of that name IS the library, and the text is located in it alone, with the same suffix rule. **A library that no place holds, or a text the library does not contain, is the compile-time error CBL3620** (GR2: the library shall be available): compilation fails, and there is no fallback from a named library to the default one and no silent omission of the COPY (GnuCOBOL's fallback to the default library, with a warning, is not followed: GR2 controls). The same at every edition. One mechanism implements all of it: `CopyProcessor.FindCopybook` over `LibraryPlaces` (the working directory, then the configured search paths) and `LocateInLibrary` (the name as spelled, then `CopybookSuffixes`); CBL3620 is reported by `CopyProcessor.ResolveOneCopy` on every path (kb/Work PB1355). The test harnesses name a golden's directory as a `--copy` path, as a user does, and the GnuCOBOL differential compiles each case as GnuCOBOL's testsuite runs `cobc`: in the case's directory, with its files at their own relative paths and each `-I DIR` passed as `--copy DIR`. | `conformance:85/pb1355_library_text_located`; `conformance:negative/pb1355-library-text-not-found`; `conformance:negative/pb1355-library-not-available`; `unit:TextManipulationStatementTests.LibraryText_APeriodInADirectoryPart_IsNotAnExtension`; `conformance-test:DiagnosticPositionTests.Copy_MainLinesStayMain_CopiedLinesNameTheCopybook` |
| DOC-A.1-46 | **Data storage — possible representations when the implementation provides multiple ways of storing data**, §8.5.1.1, conditionally required (the condition HOLDS: WiseOwl COBOL stores data in several usages) + documented | **The complete specification is the USAGE clause's: ONE fixed representation per usage, no per-compilation-unit option, each pinned in full by its own row.** **DISPLAY** — one byte per character position (item 209). A position holds a UTF-16 code unit (items 33 and 188); a code unit above U+00FF has no one-byte image, and at EVERY byte channel (a record written to a file, the storage image seen through a REDEFINES or a containing group, CONVERT's BYTE/HEX legs) it is written as `?` (0x3F) **and EC-DATA-CONVERSION is set** — item 33's decided disposition applied to every channel, never a silent substitution. **NATIONAL** — two bytes per position, UTF-16BE (item 213). **BINARY / COMPUTATIONAL / COMP / COMP-4** — big-endian two's complement of the unscaled value in 1, 2, 4 or 8 bytes by PICTURE digit count (items 205, 208); **COMP-5** has the same representation and owns its full container range (item 208). **BINARY-CHAR / -SHORT / -LONG / -DOUBLE** — 1, 2, 4, 8 bytes, big-endian two's complement (items 206, 207). **PACKED-DECIMAL / COMP-3** — BCD, two digits per byte, trailing sign nibble (item 215; `PIC S9(5) COMP-3` is 3 bytes). **FLOAT-SHORT / COMP-1 / FLOAT-BINARY-32** — IEEE 754 binary32, 4 bytes; **FLOAT-LONG / FLOAT-EXTENDED / COMP-2 / FLOAT-BINARY-64** — IEEE 754 binary64, 8 bytes; big-endian interchange bytes unless an endianness-phrase says otherwise (items 207, 48). **INDEX** — an 8-byte big-endian occurrence number (item 211). **POINTER, FUNCTION-POINTER, PROGRAM-POINTER, OBJECT REFERENCE** — one managed reference, 8 bytes by `FUNCTION BYTE-LENGTH` (items 216, 210, 214; PROGRAM-POINTER is item 217). **BIT** — one bit per boolean position, rounded up to whole bytes (`PIC 1(8) USAGE BIT` is 1 byte, `PIC 1(9)` is 2); a boolean item of usage DISPLAY is one character position per boolean position. Figurative constants: item 74. **Not provided** (refused by name, COBOLNET1564 / COBOLNET1943): FLOAT-BINARY-128 and FLOAT-DECIMAL-16/-34 (§2, Annex A.3 items 17 and 19) and MESSAGE-TAG (A.3 item 4). | — |
| DOC-A.1-49 | **DEFINE directive — mechanism for providing the value of a compilation-variable-name from the operating environment**, §7.3.11.4 GR4, required + documented | **The compiler process's environment variable whose name is the compilation-variable-name in its canonical UPPER-CASE spelling**, read once, when the `>>DEFINE … AS PARAMETER` directive is processed. A compilation-variable-name is a COBOL word, so every spelling of one name (`MyVar`, `MYVAR`, `myvar`) reads the SAME variable, `MYVAR`, on every host, including a host whose environment names are case-sensitive. **Absent variable ⇒ the name is NOT defined** (GR4's second sentence), and a definition it had before the directive is removed. **Present variable ⇒ the name is defined with the variable's text as its value**: NUMERIC when the whole text parses as an invariant-culture decimal number (surrounding white space, a leading or trailing sign, `.` as the decimal point and `,` as a group separator are accepted — `7`, ` -2.5 `, `1,000`), otherwise ALPHANUMERIC with the text exactly as given (an empty variable is a zero-length alphanumeric value). Every read is recorded on the compilation's inputs (`CompilationInputs.EnvironmentReads`). The same lookup serves the DISPLAY directive's PARAMETER operand (item 55). | `unit:ConditionalCompilationDefineTests.Parameter_TwoSpellingsOfOneName_ReadOneVariable`; `unit:ConditionalCompilationDefineTests.Parameter_ValueCategory_FollowsTheDocumentedTyping`; `unit:ConditionalCompilationDefineTests.Parameter_Unavailable_NotDefined`; `unit:ConditionalCompilationDefineTests.Parameter_SourcesFromEnvironment` |
| DOC-A.1-52 | **Devices that allow concurrent access**, §9.1.15 Sharing mode, required + documented | **All of them — the same determination as item 76, which answers the same §9.1.15 sentence.** Every physical file is a path in the host file system (a regular file, or a device path such as `/dev/null` that the host opens as a file), and every such path allows concurrent access: the SHARING clause and the SHARING phrase always apply, and a second connector's OPEN of the same file is decided by §9.1.15 Table 19 without testing the device (`FileRegistry.SharedOpenAttempt`, `PhysicalFileTable`). `ASSIGN TO PRINTER` and `ASSIGN TO DISK` name the same shareable file (item 71). This row states which devices allow concurrent access and nothing more: how strongly the file lock excludes ANOTHER run unit is §9.1.15's lock rule, open under kb/Work PB833, and the sharing mode used when none is specified is item 77, open under kb/Work PB322. | `conformance:2002/l1c11_sharing_every_device_shareable` |
| DOC-A.1-53 | **DISPLAY directive — conversion of data transferred**, §7.3.12.4 GR1 (with GR2, GR4), required + documented | **Each operand is converted to a character image and the images of one directive are written as ONE line to the compiler's standard output, at compile time, during text manipulation.** The compiler produces no source listing (item 117), so GR2 makes the result the implementor's; WiseOwl COBOL follows GnuCOBOL (CLAUDE.md rule 1 precedence), which writes the operands to the compiler's standard output when the directive is processed. Conversion: an **alphanumeric or national literal** transfers its characters without the delimiters and with no other change; a **boolean literal or boolean-expression-1** transfers its value as the boolean characters `0` and `1`; an **arithmetic-expression-1** (a numeric literal included) is evaluated in compile-time arithmetic and transfers its value in the shortest decimal form: a leading `-` only when negative, at least one integer digit, and a `.` followed by the fraction digits only when the value is not an integer, with no trailing zeros (`42`, `-2.5`, `0.125`); a **PARAMETER** operand transfers the environment value's text unchanged (item 55). The images are joined in operand order (GR4) with ONE space between them and the line ends with the host newline — `>>DISPLAY "HELLO-COMPILE" 42` writes `HELLO-COMPILE 42`. With no UPON phrase (GR6's UPON LISTING) the line goes to standard output; UPON compile-time-device-1 is item 54. Nothing is added to the compiled program's own output. | `unit:DisplayDirectiveTests.ConversionOfData_FollowsTheDeterminedImages`; `unit:DisplayDirectiveTests.OperandsAreTransferredInOrderAsOneLine`; `conformance:2023/pb807_display_directive_operands` |
| DOC-A.1-54 | **DISPLAY directive — meaning of compile-time-device-1**, §7.3.12.4 GR5 b), required + documented | **compile-time-device-1 is one of the OUTPUT-capable device-names of the SPECIAL-NAMES table (item 189), naming the same stream in the compiler process:** `CONSOLE` and `SYSOUT` — the compiler's standard output; `SYSERR` — the compiler's standard error. `SYSIN` (input only) and any other word are a compile-time error naming the available devices, from the one `ImplementorNames` table, so the directive and a SPECIAL-NAMES entry can never disagree about what a device-name means. `UPON LISTING` (GR5 a) goes to the device used for source listings; no listing is produced (item 117), so that device is the compiler's standard output, the same place as an omitted UPON phrase (GR6, item 53). An UPON phrase naming several devices, or devices and LISTING (§5.2.6.4 choice indicators, each at most once), writes the line once to each named destination, in the order written. | `unit:DisplayDirectiveTests.Upon_NamesTheDestinations`; `unit:DisplayDirectiveTests.Upon_BreakingItsFormat_IsDiagnosedAndTransfersNothing`; `conformance:negative/pb807-display-directive-upon-input-device`; `conformance:negative/pb807-display-directive-upon-listing-twice` |
| DOC-A.1-55 | **DISPLAY directive — mechanism for providing the value of a compilation-variable-name from the operating environment**, §7.3.12.4 GR3, required + documented | **The same mechanism as the DEFINE directive's PARAMETER phrase (item 49): the compiler process's environment variable whose name is the compilation-variable-name in its canonical UPPER-CASE spelling**, read when the DISPLAY directive is processed, so `PARAMETER MyVar` and `PARAMETER MYVAR` read the same variable on every host. A present variable's text is transferred unchanged, with no numeric conversion (item 53); an empty variable transfers an empty image. **When the variable is absent, that DISPLAY directive transfers nothing at all** — no line is written for any of its operands (GR3: "no transfer shall take place"). The name is not thereby defined as a compilation variable; only a DEFINE directive defines one. | `unit:DisplayDirectiveTests.Parameter_IsReadFromTheEnvironment_ByTheUpperCaseName`; `unit:DisplayDirectiveTests.AbsentParameter_TransfersNothingAtAll` |
| DOC-A.1-60 | **Dynamic-capacity table — determination of the highest permissible occurrence number**, §8.4.2.3.4 GR2 (with §8.5.1.9.6 GR2), required + need not be documented (VOLUNTARY — kept because item 62 and item 61 depend on it) | **The SMALLER of 1,073,741,823 (`0x3FFFFFFF`, `CobolDynTable.MaxOccurrences`) and the largest capacity the process can allocate at the moment the table grows**, for every dimension of every `OCCURS DYNAMIC` table, in every edition that has them. The constant is one fixed number for every element type and size, about HALF the .NET array-length limit (`Array.MaxLength` = 2,147,483,591 = `0x7FFFFFC7`), chosen so the doubling growth of item 61, clamped at the constant, never overflows a 32-bit length; the resource bound is what GR2's "may be affected by the availability of runtime resources" names, and it is reached when the managed heap cannot hold the grown table (item 61: each table is one managed array, grown by doubling). A growth past either bound — implicit, through a receiving reference, or explicit, through `SET … TO` — sets the **fatal EC-BOUND-TABLE-LIMIT** (§8.5.1.9.6 GR2) and leaves the capacity unchanged (§14.9.39.4 GR30); it never ends the run unit with a host out-of-memory failure that no COBOL exception condition names (`CobolDynTable.GrowTo` / `Enlarged` / `FromCurrentImage`; kb/Work PB1410). **The same constant is the maximum permissible value of integer-4 and integer-5** of Format 4 (§13.18.38.3 SR29: "The implementor shall specify a maximum permissible value for integer-4 and integer-5"): an `OCCURS DYNAMIC` FROM or TO value above 1,073,741,823 is refused at compile time, COBOLNET2647 (`DataBinder.DynamicBoundWithinMaximum`; kb/Work PB1264). | `unit:CobolDynTableTests.SetCapacity_BeyondRuntimeResources_LeavesTheCapacityUnchanged`; `conformance:2023/pb463_dyn_implementor_maximum` |
| DOC-A.1-63 | **Dynamic-length elementary items — structure when no dynamic-length-structure-name is specified**, §8.5.1.10.2 / §13.18.19.3 SR3, required + need not be documented (VOLUNTARY — kept because it decides what a record holding such items looks like) | **A native .NET `System.String` holding the item's current content, one UTF-16 code unit per character position, with NO length field and NO delimiter**: the current length is the string's own length, from 0 up to the maximum size (item 62). The item is located outside the record it belongs to (§8.5.1.10.3 permits this; the record holds a reference). **In a record image** it contributes exactly its current content (each character position in item 209's or item 213's representation), and a record that contains dynamic-length items is written with its extent table beside it on the storage medium (§3 D-FRA (v)): each variable-length member's offset and current length, which is not part of the record area or the record size. So a READ gives every member back at the length it was written with, even when two dynamic-length items are next to each other: `D1` = `ABC` and `D2` = `DE` written as one record read back as `ABC` (length 3) and `DE` (length 2). **In a file of FIXED-length records (an explicit RECORD clause Format 1) there is no frame to carry the table, so the record is carried in its FIXED FORM instead — each member at its maximum width, space padded, and read back without the trailing padding (§3 D-FRA (vi); `conformance:2014/pb1562_fixed_clause_file_second_description`).** A structure named by a DYNAMIC LENGTH STRUCTURE clause (PREFIXED / DELIMITED) is §3 D-DL3, not this row. | `conformance:2014/pb1053_dynamic_members_both_sides` |
| DOC-A.1-66 | **External repository — mechanism for specifying whether checking and updating occur**, §8.13, required + documented | **The external repository is the metadata of compiled WiseOwl COBOL assemblies** (owner decision kb/Work R44): every program, function, class and interface assembly the compiler produces carries its source units' §8.13 signature information as .NET metadata, with a WiseOwl COBOL-owned metadata record for whatever .NET metadata cannot express. A `cobol` command option names the repository assemblies a compilation references; REPOSITORY-paragraph specifiers resolve against those assemblies and the compilation group's own definitions and prototypes, and a name that resolves to neither is diagnosed. **Updating** (§8.13's first mechanism): a compilation updates the repository by emitting that metadata into the assembly it produces, and a user-visible option chooses whether it does. **Checking** (§8.13's second mechanism): a user-visible option chooses whether differences between a prototype or definition in the compilation group and the signature recorded in a referenced repository assembly are flagged. The options' spellings and defaults are fixed by the R44 design doc (not yet written; kb/Work PB1086), which fills them into this row in the same change set. | — |
| DOC-A.1-67 | **External repository — information beyond the required information**, §8.13, optional (documented if provided) | **The external repository is the metadata of compiled WiseOwl COBOL assemblies** (owner decision kb/Work R44, 2026-09-24): the §8.13 information of each source unit is carried as .NET metadata, with a WiseOwl COBOL-owned metadata record for whatever .NET metadata cannot express. Whether that record holds anything beyond the §8.13 list (“any other information that the implementor requires”), and what, is fixed by the R44 design doc (kb/Work PB1086), which writes it into this row in the same change set. ⚠ Not yet implemented: no repository exists today, REPOSITORY-paragraph specifiers are never resolved against one (`CLASS NOSUCHCL PROGRAM NOSUCHPG` compiles and runs clean), and the compiler has no option to reference or update a repository — kb/Work PB1086. | — |
| DOC-A.1-68 | **Externalized names — formation and mapping rules**, §7.3.9.3 GR2 a) and b), §8.3.2.2, required + documented | **Formation.** An externalized name is a string of characters. For a name written as a COBOL word with no AS phrase (a program-name, a user-function-name, a class-name, an interface-name, an EXTERNAL data-name or file-name) it is the word's spelling. For a name given by an `AS literal` it is the literal's content with **leading and trailing spaces removed**; spaces inside the name are kept. A CALL, CANCEL, `ADDRESS OF PROGRAM` or `ADDRESS OF FUNCTION` target and an INVOKE method name are formed the same way: a literal's content, or an identifier's content, with leading and trailing spaces removed. One function forms and maps every one of these names, at bind time and at run time alike (`CobolNet.Runtime.ExternalizedNames`). An AS literal made only of spaces forms the zero-length name, which nothing can name, so it is refused (COBOLNET2643) wherever the clause itself refuses a zero-length literal: every AS clause except CLASS-ID, whose §11.3.3 SR1 admits one. ISO leaves formation to the implementor (§8.3.2.2). GnuCOBOL removes leading and trailing spaces, with a warning, from a CALL or CANCEL literal and from a literal program-name, but keeps an AS literal as written, so a GnuCOBOL program named `AS "trail  "` cannot be called. WiseOwl COBOL removes them from AS names too, so that every externalized name is formed by one rule, and gives a compile-time warning (COBOLNET2642) when an AS literal has leading or trailing spaces. **Mapping.** Two externalized names are the same name when they are equal ignoring case: `CALL "sub-p"` and `CALL "SUB-P"` both reach `PROGRAM-ID. SUB-P`, and `EXTERNAL AS "Shared Two"` and `EXTERNAL AS "shared two"` are one data item. A program with an AS phrase is reached by its AS name, never by its declared word. **CALL-CONVENTION (§7.3.9).** WiseOwl COBOL defines no call-convention-name. `>>CALL-CONVENTION COBOL` (the default, §7.3.9.3 GR1) is the only accepted form, so GR2 a) always applies: program-names and method-names in CALL, CANCEL, INVOKE and program-address-identifiers map by the COBOL-word rule above. `>>CALL-CONVENTION` followed by any other word is a compile-time error (COBOLNET1911), because that word names no convention and therefore no GR2 b) literal mapping (kb/Work PB1383). Because the invocation side can only ever be under COBOL and a method's ENTRY-CONVENTION can only be COBOL (any entry-convention-name-1 is COBOLNET2385, A.1 item 64), §9.3.6 match rule 2 ("The same calling convention shall be in effect for the invocation as is in effect for the invoked method") holds for every INVOKE by construction. | `conformance:2002/pb303_as_externalized_name`; `conformance:2002/w73d_pb1539_as_name_formation`; `conformance:negative/w73d-pb1539-as-literal-all-spaces`; `conformance:2002/pb1383_call_convention_cobol`; `conformance:negative/pb1383-call-convention-undefined-name` |
| DOC-A.1-75 | **File sharing — interaction with other facilities and languages**, §9.1.15, optional (documented if provided) | **Provided: other facilities see COBOL file sharing only as a host lock on the physical file.** When an OPEN succeeds, the connector takes a host lock on the physical file chosen from BOTH its sharing mode (§9.1.15 rules 1–3) and its open mode, so that another opener — in this run unit, in another WiseOwl COBOL run unit, or in another language that honours the lock — is admitted or refused exactly as §9.1.15 Table 19 decides. A connector with no sharing mode specified locks as the implementor-default mode (item 77). The lock lasts until the connector is closed. The reverse direction also holds: when the host refuses a WiseOwl COBOL OPEN because another process, in any language, holds the file under an incompatible lock or share mode, the OPEN fails with I-O status **61** (§9.1.13.9 1)); a DELETE FILE refused the same way gets **62**. **On Windows** the lock is the host share mode of the connector's own handle, which is mandatory and per-access, so every process on the machine is bound by rules 1–3; the one thing a share mode cannot refuse is the truncation by an `OPEN OUTPUT` (Table 19 prints *Unsuccessful open* in every OUTPUT cell), so an `OPEN OUTPUT` on an existing file first requests exclusive access of the host and is refused with **61** if any handle holds it, before anything is truncated (on Linux the region lock refuses the OUTPUT like every other cell). **On Linux (x86-64 and arm64, kernel 3.15 or later, a filesystem that holds byte-range locks)** the connector also holds an advisory open-file-description byte-range lock (`fcntl` `F_OFD_SETLK`) on the physical file — its Table 19 column, published as a shared lock on one byte far beyond any record — and, before the OPEN succeeds, tests the five columns its request row refuses for another holder (`F_OFD_GETLK`), so every WiseOwl COBOL run unit is arbitrated by §9.1.15 Table 19 across processes as it is inside one; the lock is removed by the CLOSE (or an unsuccessful OPEN) and dies with a run unit that terminates. Only a regular file is locked: a device node such as `/dev/null` carries no region lock (it is not a file whose bytes another run unit can damage, and the lock would otherwise arbitrate every run unit on the machine that assigns it). The share mode (`flock`) is still taken on the connector's own handle, so rule 1 also excludes an outside process that takes `flock`. **A program in another language, or one that opens the file without taking either lock, is neither stopped nor detected** except by the share mode's strength on that host (on Linux and macOS a shared `flock`, which refuses a writer only under NO OTHER); a program in another language is bound by rules 2 and 3 only if it takes the same region locks (the byte layout is `RunUnitFileLock`'s). **On macOS** (and any Unix whose kernel has no open-file-description locks, or whose filesystem has no byte-range locks) the region lock is unavailable and a run unit meets only the share mode, so rule 2 is not refused across run units there; the process-owned locks macOS offers cannot stand in (closing any descriptor of the file drops them). Design: `docs/rearchitecture/DESIGN-runtime-library.md`; witness `unit:RunUnitFileLockDriftTests` (every sharing × open mode pair, in a second run unit). | `unit:RunUnitFileLockDriftTests.ASecondRunUnitsOpenIsArbitratedByTable19`; `unit:FileLockPostureDriftTests.TheHostsShareModeSemanticsAreTheDocumentedOnes` |
| DOC-A.1-77 | **File sharing — the default sharing mode when none is specified**, §9.1.15, §14.9.27.4 GR23 (and §9.1.3), required + documented | **It depends on the open mode.** When neither the OPEN statement's SHARING phrase nor the file control entry's SHARING clause gives a mode, the OPEN establishes one of §9.1.15's own modes for the connector: **`OPEN INPUT` opens `SHARING WITH READ ONLY`**, and **`OPEN OUTPUT`, `OPEN I-O` and `OPEN EXTEND` open `SHARING WITH NO OTHER`**. The mode lasts until the connector is closed, and every other connector is arbitrated against it by §9.1.15 Table 19, whether that connector is in this run unit or in another one. So with two clause-less connectors on one file: `OPEN INPUT` and a second `OPEN INPUT` both succeed with 00; `OPEN INPUT` followed by a second connector's `OPEN I-O`, `OPEN EXTEND` or `OPEN OUTPUT` fails with 61; and any OPEN of a file that a clause-less connector holds open in OUTPUT, I-O or EXTEND mode fails with 61. The host lock for these modes is described in item 75. This follows GnuCOBOL, which locks a file shared for input and exclusive for every other mode; ISO leaves the choice to the implementor (CLAUDE.md rule 1). This row covers COBOL 2002, 2014 and 2023. The COBOL-85 behaviour, where the 6x statuses do not exist, is kb/Work PB670. Implemented by `FileRegistry.ImplementorDefaultSharing` (read once, in `FileRegistry.SharedOpenAttempt`) and `Table19.Conflicts`, a plain lookup of the printed table. | `conformance:2002/pb322_default_sharing_by_open_mode`; `unit:OpenTable19Tests.ImplementorDefault_IsAFunctionOfTheOpenMode` |
| DOC-A.1-81 | **Floating-point numeric item — alignment when used as a receiving operand**, §14.6.8.3 rule 1, required — documentation NOT required by A.1 (VOLUNTARY) | **One correctly rounded conversion to the receiver's own format.** The sending operand's algebraic value (for a decimal sender, every digit it holds) is converted in ONE step to the nearest value the receiver can represent. Ties go to even, ISO/IEC 60559 round-to-nearest. `FLOAT-SHORT` and `COMP-1` hold IEEE binary32. `FLOAT-LONG`, `FLOAT-EXTENDED` and `COMP-2` hold binary64 (item 207). `FLOAT-BINARY-32`/`-64` convert the same way, as §14.6.8.3 rule 2 requires. The value is never rounded first to a wider binary format and then narrowed. A value too small for the normal range becomes the nearest subnormal, or zero when it is closer to zero than half the smallest subnormal. A value too large for the format is the §14.9.25.4 GR6 d)4.a case covered in §3 ("EC-DATA-NOT-FINITE / EC-DATA-OVERFLOW applied to EVERY floating-point usage"): with checking off the result is ±Infinity, and with EC-DATA-OVERFLOW checking on the exception condition is raised. So `MOVE 0.1 TO` a FLOAT-SHORT stores 0.100000001490116…, and `MOVE 1.000000059604644775390625000001 TO` a FLOAT-SHORT stores 1.00000011920928955 (the binary32 neighbour nearer the value), not 1. This holds for EVERY statement that lands a value in a binary32 receiver: MOVE, a ROUNDED arithmetic resultant, INITIALIZE, the VALUE clause and the ACCEPT device transfer. Two statements round by their own rule rather than to nearest: an arithmetic resultant with no ROUNDED phrase truncates (§14.7.4.3 rule 2; a CALL BY CONTENT or BY VALUE crossing into a binary32 formal lands through that same no-ROUNDED store, so it truncates too), and a value produced by a function (a NUMVAL-F result is a binary64 approximation, §15.69.4 rule 2) is converted as that binary64 sender. Each is still ONE rounding of the value it receives (kb/Work PB1110). | `conformance:2023/pb271_float_receiver_overflow` (out-of-range half); `conformance:2023/pb1110_binary32_receiver_single_rounding`; `conformance:2002/pb1110_float_short_every_landing` |
| DOC-A.1-89 | **Function-identifier — the runtime resources checked before a user-defined function is executed**, §8.4.3.2.4 GR6 c), required + documented | **The set of runtime resources checked is EMPTY, so a function activation never raises EC-PROGRAM-RESOURCES** — the determination item 14 makes for CALL, applied to a user-defined function. WiseOwl COBOL checks no runtime resource beyond locating the function (GR6 b): a function is located when its externalized name (item 68) matches a function registered in the run unit — compiled into the calling assembly, or a separately compiled module `<name>.dll` beside the application whose `__CobolModule.Register()` registrar has run (`ProgramTable.ProbeSiblingModule`, the lookup CALL uses). A module file that exists but cannot be loaded (a truncated or foreign image, or a dependency the registrar itself needs) or whose registrar throws has therefore NOT been located: **EC-FUNCTION-NOT-FOUND** is set, the function activation is not successful, and execution continues as GR6 f) specifies. **Available memory and activation depth are not checked**: running out of either is a host failure, the run unit ends abnormally and no exception condition is set. This follows GnuCOBOL under CLAUDE.md rule 1's precedence — libcob's resolver sets EC-FUNCTION-NOT-FOUND for a function module that is found but cannot be loaded (`call.c` `set_resolve_error`) and has no EC-PROGRAM-RESOURCES raise site. | `conformance-test:FunctionLocateResourceWitnessTests.UnloadableSiblingModule_IsNotFound_NeverProgramResources`; `conformance-test:FunctionLocateResourceWitnessTests.LoadableSiblingModule_IsLocatedAndRuns`; `conformance-test:FunctionLocateResourceWitnessTests.ThrowingRegistrarSiblingModule_IsNotFound_NeverProgramResources` |
| DOC-A.1-99 | **Implementor-defined exception conditions, EC-IMP-*suffix* — specification and meaning of each suffix**, §14.6.13.1.6 Table 13 (§14.6.13.1.1, the 'EC-IMP-' type), optional (documented if provided) | **Not provided.** WiseOwl COBOL specifies no EC-IMP-*suffix* exception-name. The level-2 name **EC-IMP** is still valid wherever Table 13 allows a level-2 name, and no level-3 condition exists under it. The EC-level-2-IMP names of item 100, such as EC-PROGRAM-IMP, fall under their own level-2 parents, not under EC-IMP. `EC-IMP-` followed by any suffix names no exception condition, so a program that writes one is refused at compile time with COBOLNET0711: in `RAISE EXCEPTION`, in a `>>TURN` directive (reported at the directive's line) and in a `USE AFTER EXCEPTION CONDITION` declarative. `ExceptionCatalog.TryGet` answers only the catalogued names and the user-defined `EC-USER-suffix` family, which §14.6.13.1.1 makes open by definition ("The name is defined by specifying it"). The implementation never sets an EC-IMP-*suffix* condition at run time. The standard still reserves the EC-IMP-*suffix* spelling in compiler directives (§8.12), so `CompilerDirectiveWords.IsReserved` tests the spelling (`ExceptionCatalog.IsImplementorSuffixForm`) although no such name resolves. | `conformance:negative/pb1531-ec-imp-suffix-raise`; `conformance:negative/pb1531-ec-imp-suffix-turn`; `conformance:negative/pb1531-ec-imp-suffix-use`; `conformance:2002/l1_dns_a1_99_ec_imp_level2_valid`; `unit:EcImplementorNamesDocumentationDriftTests.Item99_NoEcImpSuffixNameExists` |
| DOC-A.1-102 | **INVOKE statement — the runtime resources checked before a method is executed**, §14.9.23.4 GR7 b) — Annex A.1 cites GR7 e), but the resource sentence is GR7 b); required + documented | **One resource is checked: that the class which provides the method can be loaded and initialized.** When §9.3.6 resolution finds the method, the INVOKE checks that the class's type initialization completes. That initialization runs when the class's factory object is first referenced in the run unit (§9.3.14.2: the factory object is created before it is first referenced; `RunUnit.FactoryObject`), and it covers the class's static data. If it fails, **EC-OO-METHOD** is set (fatal, Table 13), the method invocation fails, and execution continues as GR7 g) specifies. The CLR remembers the failure, so every later INVOKE of that class fails the same way. The standard gives this outcome to both "method not found" and "resources not available". **Loading the class's assembly is not a checked resource.** A class a program can INVOKE is compiled into its own compilation group's assembly, or comes from a referenced repository assembly (item 67), and the host binds that assembly when it first prepares the INVOKE's enclosing method for execution, before any statement of that method runs. A binding failure is a deployment failure the host reports, and no exception condition is set. **Available memory and activation depth are not checked.** Running out of either is a host failure: the run unit ends abnormally and no exception condition is set. The CALL twin (item 14) and the function twin (item 89) count a module that cannot be loaded as NOT LOCATED rather than as a resource failure; for INVOKE the distinction has no observable effect, because GR7 b) gives both outcomes EC-OO-METHOD. EC-OO-METHOD is raised by `CobolObject.__CobolInvoke` when no class in the hierarchy declares a matching method, and by `RunUnit.FactoryObject` when the class cannot be initialized (kb/Work PB1422). | `unit:FactoryInitializationFailureTests.ClassWhoseTypeInitializerThrows_IsEcOoMethod_EveryTime`; `unit:FactoryInitializationFailureTests.ClassThatInitializes_IsTheSameFactoryObjectEveryTime` |
| DOC-A.1-103 | **I-O status — the action taken for a fatal exception condition**, §9.1.13.1 (with §14.6.13.1.3 rule 3), required + documented | **Continue only when the program is equipped to see the failure; otherwise terminate.** The fatal I-O statuses are those that begin with 3, 4 or 7, and every 9x status WiseOwl COBOL defines (item 110). The action is decided after the I-O status is stored and any applicable USE procedure has run. **With checking for the EC-I-O condition not enabled (the default):** execution **continues** if the file connector has a FILE STATUS clause or a USE procedure applied to the statement. Control passes to the end of the statement that produced the status, and its NOT AT END or NOT INVALID KEY phrase is not executed (§9.1.13.1). If the connector has **neither**, the run unit **terminates abnormally** (§14.6.12): the message names the exception-name, the I-O status, the file and the statement (`EC-I-O-PERMANENT-ERROR (fatal): I-O status 35 on F (OPEN); the file has no FILE STATUS clause and no USE procedure applies`), and the exit code is nonzero (§2 row 44). **With checking enabled** (`>>TURN EC-I-O CHECKING ON`, or a level above it), WiseOwl COBOL adds nothing: §14.6.13.1.3 rules 4–7 apply after the I-O rules. A PERFORM WHEN phrase or a RESUME in the declarative can continue execution; otherwise the run unit terminates abnormally, even when the file has a FILE STATUS clause. This follows GnuCOBOL, which ends the run unit on a fatal status only when no declarative applies and the file has no FILE STATUS clause; ISO leaves the choice to the implementor (CLAUDE.md rule 1). A host failure inside a READ or WRITE is reported as status 30 and handled by this rule the same way. Implemented at the statement: every I-O statement's after-hook (`SequentialIoEmitter.EmitUseHook`) ends in `CobolFile.TerminateOnUncoveredFatalStatus` for a file that has no FILE STATUS clause, when the status is fatal, no USE procedure ran and the statement has no ON EXCEPTION phrase; a SORT or MERGE implicit transfer follows the verb's own rule instead (§14.6.13.1.3 rule 2). A program whose files all carry FILE STATUS emits nothing new. COBOL-85 programs are held to the same rule: two official NIST programs that depended on continuing (NC401M opens a file that does not exist; ST146A's dump READs a closed file) are recorded in `tests/nist/corpus.tsv` as `divergent` with a `TERMINATES` note, and the harness asserts the termination. | `conformance-test:DocA1Item103WitnessTests.FatalStatus_NoFileStatusNoUse_TerminatesAbnormally`; `conformance-test:DocA1Item103WitnessTests.FatalLogicErrorStatus_OnWrite_NoFileStatusNoUse_TerminatesAbnormally`; `conformance-test:DocA1Item103WitnessTests.FatalStatus_WithFileStatusClause_Continues`; `conformance-test:DocA1Item103WitnessTests.FatalStatus_WithApplicableUseProcedure_Continues`; `conformance-test:DocA1Item218WitnessTests.CheckedFatalIo_Format1UseCompletesNormally_TerminatesAbnormally` |
| DOC-A.1-105 | **I-O status, permanent error — the technique for correcting it**, §9.1.13.1, optional + documented if provided | **Provided: the technique is CLOSE.** A permanent error condition (an I-O status beginning with '3', §9.1.13.6) belongs to the file connector that got it, and it stays in effect until that connector is closed. **After a failed OPEN** ('30', '31', '35', '37', '38' — COBOL-85 to 2014 only: CLOSE WITH LOCK and status '38' were removed at 2023 — or '39') the connector is left closed. That closed state is already the corrected state, so the next OPEN of the file starts fresh. **After a failed READ, WRITE, REWRITE, DELETE or START** on an open connector ('30' or '34'), the condition stays in effect. Every later input-output statement on that connector, except CLOSE, is unsuccessful. It does not touch the physical file, it sets the same I-O status again, and it raises that status's exception condition again (§9.1.13.1). A CLOSE runs normally, and a completed CLOSE — successful or not, since an unsuccessful one also leaves the connector closed — ends the condition, so the next OPEN starts clean. This applies to every organization and every edition. Example: a random relative WRITE with relative key 0 answers '34' (§14.9.51.4 GR29 b). The next WRITE, with key 1, also answers '34' and writes nothing. CLOSE answers '00', and after OPEN I-O the WRITE with key 1 answers '00'. **The condition outranks every other status the later statement would have taken** — the not-open and mode statuses ('41', '47', '48', '49'), GR21's '46' and the '43' gate — because §9.1.13.1 says it remains in effect "for all subsequent input-output operations on the file": READ, WRITE, REWRITE, DELETE, START, UNLOCK, DELETE FILE and a second OPEN of the open connector all answer the status that began it. It is started in ONE place, the I-O status assignment itself (`FileConnector.Status`'s setter: a status beginning '3' on a connector that is open), so no producer — a host failure '30', a boundary '34', a READ of an OCCURS DEPENDING ON record made too long (D-ODO2) — can forget to start it, and each verb family asks `FileConnector.PermanentErrorReplay` once in the precondition it already shares (`PermanentErrorInEffectTests` pins every organization x verb). | `conformance:2023/pb1541_permanent_error_remains_in_effect`; `unit:PermanentErrorInEffectTests.RecordSequential_EveryVerbReplaysThePermanentError_AndCloseEndsIt`; `unit:PermanentErrorInEffectTests.Indexed_EveryVerbReplaysThePermanentError_AndCloseEndsIt`; `unit:PermanentErrorInEffectTests.Relative_RegistryVerbs_ReplayTheBoundaryError_UnlockAndDeleteFileToo_AndCloseEndsIt`; `unit:PermanentErrorInEffectTests.FailedOpen_LeavesNoConditionInEffect_TheNextOpenStartsFresh` |
| DOC-A.1-107 | **I-O status 24 — the manner in which the boundaries of a relative or indexed file are defined**, §9.1.13.5 item 4 + §14.9.51.4 GR33 b) / GR42 d), required + documented | **The boundary of a physical relative or indexed file is the capacity of its store**: 2 147 483 591 bytes (`RecordFraming.MaxStoreBytes`, the largest array the store image is composed in and loaded from), counting the store header, every record's frame (its 4-byte length word, its extent table if any, one byte per character) and the 4-byte empty-slot tag of every relative record number below the highest one written. A record the store cannot hold is outside the boundary — for a 4-character relative record, RRN 536 870 891 is the last that fits an empty store and 536 870 892 the first that does not. The test runs **at the WRITE**, over the same size formula the persist composes with (`KeyedConnector.StoreHolds`): the WRITE is unsuccessful with I-O status '24' (§14.9.51.4 GR33 b) relative, GR42 d) indexed), the invalid key condition exists (so the INVALID KEY phrase runs), no record is released, and the file is unchanged — a WRITE is never reported '00' and then failed at CLOSE. A REWRITE whose longer record the store cannot hold is the same §9.1.13.5 item 4 "attempt … to write outside the externally-defined boundaries" and answers '24' likewise, before anything is replaced. **The host medium is reached at the CLOSE, not at the WRITE**: a relative or indexed file is held in memory while it is open and written whole by the CLOSE, so a host medium too full to hold the store (POSIX ENOSPC / EDQUOT / EFBIG; Windows ERROR_DISK_FULL / ERROR_HANDLE_DISK_FULL / ERROR_FILE_TOO_LARGE / ERROR_DISK_QUOTA_EXCEEDED) makes the CLOSE unsuccessful with §9.1.13.6 item 1's '30' and the physical file keeps its previous image. **This is separate from the relative key's own range.** A random or dynamic relative WRITE whose relative key is less than 1, or greater than 2 147 483 647 (the highest relative record number permitted for the file), answers '34' as §14.9.51.4 GR29 b) states, and takes no INVALID KEY branch. GR29 b) names that case explicitly, so it governs over the general GR33 b); GnuCOBOL's '24' for key 0 is not followed because the standard decides it. A key from the store's capacity up to 2 147 483 647 is a permitted relative record number that does not fit: '24'. On a sequential-access WRITE, a relative record number with more significant digits than the relative key answers '24' (§14.9.51.4 GR33 c), §9.1.13.5 item 4); in random or dynamic access the record number comes from the key, so it cannot happen. Same in every edition. | `conformance:85/pb1192_relative_write_boundary_85`; `unit:FileBoundaryTests.RelativeWrite_BoundaryIsTheStoreCapacity_AndTheKeyRangeIs34` |
| DOC-A.1-108 | **I-O status 34 — the manner in which the boundaries of a sequential file are defined**, §9.1.13.6 item 3 + §14.9.51.4 GR20, required + documented | **The boundary of a physical sequential file (record sequential or line sequential) is the capacity of the host medium.** A WRITE is outside the boundary when the host cannot store the record because the medium is full or a host quota or file-size limit is reached (POSIX ENOSPC / EDQUOT / EFBIG; Windows ERROR_DISK_FULL / ERROR_HANDLE_DISK_FULL). The failure is detected at the WRITE (`HostFile.IsMediumBoundary` classifies the host's refusal; `FileStatusCode.ForWriteFailure` answers the WRITE, WRITE ADVANCING and REWRITE entry points of `SequentialConnector`). The writer buffers records — a flush per record is paid only when another connector may read the file — so the refusal is reported on the WRITE whose record the host is asked to store; a CLOSE whose final flush is refused answers '30' (§9.1.13.6 item 1). The WRITE is unsuccessful with I-O status '34', a permanent error (EC-I-O-PERMANENT-ERROR). No part of the record counts as written, and the file stays open. Under item 105 the condition then stays in effect on that connector until it is closed. Any other host failure during a WRITE is '30' (§9.1.13.6 item 1), never an escaped .NET exception. No other sequential boundary exists: there is no record-count or size limit below the medium's. Same in every edition. | `unit:FileBoundaryTests.SequentialWrite_OnAFullMedium_Is34_AtTheWrite`; `unit:FileBoundaryTests.WriteFailure_MediumExhaustionIs34_AnyOtherFailureIs30` |
| DOC-A.1-113 | **Life cycle for objects — timing and algorithm for deciding that an object can no longer take part in continued execution**, §9.3.14.3, required (A.1 does not require it to be documented; documented here because it decides when §9.1.4's implicit CLOSE of an object's files happens) | **Reachability, decided by the .NET garbage collector.** An instance object is a managed instance of a class derived from `CobolObject`. It can no longer take part in continued execution once no object reference in the run unit reaches it. The .NET tracing garbage collector decides that at a time of its own choosing, which is nondeterministic, as §9.3.14.3 permits. When it has decided, the object is destroyed. Its finalizer implicitly CLOSEs every per-object file connector the object owns (§9.1.4, 'when the object is deleted'), **in the run unit that created the object**, and the object's storage is reclaimed. A later OPEN of the same physical file is therefore arbitrated only against connectors that are still live. At run-unit termination every object not yet destroyed is destroyed, and its files are closed with the run unit's (`CobolFile.CloseAll`). Whichever of the two happens first is the destruction. An object that owns no file has no observable destruction beyond its storage being reclaimed. COBOL 2002 and later. | `unit:InstanceFileFinalizerTests.AnUnreachableObjectsFile_IsClosedInItsOwnRunUnit_BeforeTheNextOpen` |
| DOC-A.1-114 | **Line delimiter — specification of**, §12.4.5.10.3 GR2 (+ §9.1.13.2 item 5's '06'), required + documented | **The line delimiter is the host platform's newline:** CR LF (U+000D U+000A) on Windows and LF (U+000A) on Linux and macOS (`Environment.NewLine`). **WRITE** ends every record with that delimiter, and ADVANCING and LINAGE positioning add their own line and page controls around it. **READ** ends a record at an LF, or at a CR LF pair on any host, so a file written on either platform reads back on the other. A final line with no delimiter ends at end of file. **A lone CR (one not followed by LF) is not a delimiter.** It is record data, and because CR is outside the line sequential character set (item 115), the READ that delivers it answers '09'. A line longer than the record area is delivered truncated with '06' (§9.1.13.2), and the rest of the line is the next record. This follows GnuCOBOL, which writes a line sequential file in the host's text mode and drops a CR only when an LF follows it; ISO leaves the delimiter to the implementor (CLAUDE.md rule 1; kb/Work PB1540). Implemented once, in `SequentialConnector` (`LineSequentialDelimiter` for every line end it writes, `ReadPhysicalLine` for every line it reads). COBOL 2023 (ORGANIZATION LINE SEQUENTIAL). | `conformance:2023/pb1540_ls_lone_cr_is_data`; `conformance-test:LinageConformanceTests.Bytes_LineSequential_EveryPlacementMix`; `conformance:2023/pb964_ls_plain_write_after_after` |
| DOC-A.1-116 | **Linkage section — whether access to linkage items is guaranteed, and their initial value, when a non-COBOL runtime element activates the program**, §13.7.4 GR3 + GR5, conditionally required (a .NET host can activate a WiseOwl COBOL program) + documented | **The only non-COBOL activator is a .NET host (item 141), and the answer depends on which entry it uses.** **Through `ICobolProgram.Call(CobolArg[] args, CobolArg? returning)`** (or `ProgramRegistry.CallProgram`), access IS guaranteed, exactly as for a COBOL CALL with a USING phrase. Each supplied argument maps positionally to the formal parameter it matches, and the formal's initial value is the content of the host's carrier (the host's own storage, item 141). The RETURNING item is delivered into the `returning` carrier. A formal the host omits (a `ManagedPointer.Null` carrier, or fewer arguments) is OMITTED, with item 141's rules. **Through `ICobolProgram.Activate()`** (the main-program entry, no arguments), access is NOT guaranteed, as for an activation by the operating system. Each formal then refers to a cell private to the program instance. On the instance's first activation that cell holds the item's class default: spaces for an alphanumeric, national, edited, group or display-numeric item; zero for a native numeric item; NULL for a pointer. After an earlier `Call` on the same instance, the formal still refers to the carrier that call bound. The formal is not OMITTED, and the RETURNING item is delivered nowhere. Same in every edition. | — |
| DOC-A.1-121 | **METHOD-ID paragraph — the actual method-name used when the PROPERTY phrase is specified**, §11.7.4 GR1 a), required + documented | **`__GET_<P>` for a GET PROPERTY method and `__SET_<P>` for a SET PROPERTY method.** `<P>` is the property-name in upper case with each hyphen replaced by an underscore: `GET PROPERTY my-bal` is `__GET_MY_BAL`. The rule is the same for every legal property-name, including one spelled like a .NET keyword: `GET PROPERTY long` is `__GET_LONG`, never an escaped or altered spelling. It is the name of the public virtual method on the generated .NET class, so a .NET caller invokes the accessor by that name. Override, interface-conformance and inheritance checks match accessors by it. A COBOL program reaches the property through an object-property reference (`long OF obj`, which needs `PROPERTY long` in the REPOSITORY paragraph), not by spelling the accessor name. COBOL 2002 and later. | `conformance:2002/oo_property_methods`; `conformance:2002/oo_property_ref` |
| DOC-A.1-124 | **Native arithmetic — when an operand represents an integer**, §5.5 3) a), required + documented | **An operand represents an integer exactly when its value has no nonzero digit to the right of the decimal point.** The test uses the operand's own exact value, never a rounded or truncated copy. A fixed-point data item is tested at its full declared scale. An arithmetic expression is tested on the value the native intermediate data item (item 123) holds for it. A floating-point operand is an integer only when its binary value is exactly integral: COMP-2 `2.0` is an integer, `2 + 1.0E-10` is not. The same value gets the same answer at every rule that asks: a subscript, a reference-modification start or length, a SET index or amount, and an integer argument of an intrinsic function. A subscript or reference-modification bound that is an arithmetic expression or a floating-point item is evaluated once, before the statement, and tested on the carrier it evaluated on — the exact scaled value, the SDIDI under standard arithmetic, or binary64 — before its integer position is stored (`CobolTable.OccValue*` / `CobolString.RefModValue*`, kb/Work PB1890; it used to be stored into a 9-fraction-digit temporary first, which hid a fraction past the 9th digit). When checking for the rule's exception condition is enabled, a non-integer is refused by that condition (for example EC-BOUND-SUBSCRIPT; for an intrinsic function's integer argument, §15.3 type 6, it is EC-ARGUMENT-FUNCTION — every carrier of the operand asks the test, the exact scaled value, the SDIDI and binary64, narrow and wide, kb/Work PB1526) and, with that checking off, the truncated integer is the result the implementor defines (§15.3's last sentence; a reference-modification position is the exception — an unchecked EC-BOUND-REF-MOD violation terminates the run unit, owner decision R60). The compile-time screen of §5.5 2) is separate (COBOLNET1627) and fails open on an expression it cannot prove non-integral (`FACTORIAL(X / 2 + 1)`), which is why the VALUE is tested at run time. Same in every edition. | `conformance:2023/pb1526_integer_argument_has_integral_value`; `conformance:2023/pb1526_integer_argument_integrality_standard_decimal`; `conformance:2023/pb1890_position_expression_exact`; `conformance:2023/pb1890_position_expression_standard_decimal`; `conformance:2023/pb41_position_not_integer`; `unit:IntrinsicCarrierAgreementDriftTests.TheIntegerIntake_RaisesOnAFractionOnEveryCarrier_AndAcceptsAnIntegralValue` |
| DOC-A.1-131 | **OPEN statement with no SHARING phrase and no SHARING clause — the sharing mode established for the file connector**, §14.9.27.4 GR23 (+ §9.1.15), required + documented | **The default sharing mode depends on the open mode.** OPEN INPUT establishes **SHARING WITH READ ONLY**. OPEN OUTPUT, OPEN I-O and OPEN EXTEND establish **SHARING WITH NO OTHER**. Both are the standard's own modes, so §9.1.15's rules and Table 19 apply to them unchanged. Clause-less readers can share a file. A clause-less updater or writer has the file to itself: its OPEN is refused with '61' while any other connector has the file open, and while it is open every other connector's OPEN is refused with '61'. A clause-less OPEN INPUT is refused with '61' while another connector has the file open in a mode other than input. The same default applies to a file control entry that has a LOCK MODE clause but no SHARING clause, and to an OPEN whose only sharing-related phrase is RETRY. Record locking for such a connector is item 153. Example (three clause-less SELECTs on one file): A OPEN INPUT '00', B OPEN INPUT '00', C OPEN I-O '61', C OPEN EXTEND '61', C OPEN OUTPUT '61'. After A and B close: A OPEN I-O '00', B OPEN INPUT '61', C OPEN I-O '61'. COBOL 2002 and later (the COBOL-85 posture is kb/Work PB670). | `conformance:2002/pb322_default_sharing_by_open_mode`; `conformance:2002/pb322_clauseless_reader_sees_record_lock`; `unit:OpenTable19Tests.ImplementorDefault_IsAFunctionOfTheOpenMode` |
| DOC-A.1-146 | **RECORD clause — the calculations to derive the size of records on the storage medium**, §13.18.43.4 GR2, required + documented | **Start from the record size in bytes (GR1/GR3/GR8)**: every alphanumeric, alphabetic, numeric-display or edited character position is ONE byte (item 209: ISO/IEC 8859-1, owner decision kb/Work R47 — a record holding a character above U+00FF is refused, item 31), a national character position TWO bytes (UTF-16BE, D-N1), and a binary, packed or floating item its item-205–215 width; the implicit or explicit RECORD clause then fixes the size (implicit clause: item 147; format 3: item 148). **Then add the control information of the file's organization:** **(a) record sequential, fixed-length records** (format 1, explicit or implied) — **exactly integer-1 bytes per record, no control information**: a record description shorter than integer-1 is written followed by the rest of the integer-1-byte record area — positions that no record description of the file covers are spaces (national spaces in a national record area; `FileModel.RecordWidth` sizes the area, `FileConnector.FitRecord` fills it) — so the file is `n × integer-1` bytes (`FD F RECORD CONTAINS 20 CHARACTERS` + `01 R PIC X(10)` → 20 bytes per record; an implied format 1 over `01 A PIC X(5)` and `01 B PIC X(10)` → 10 bytes per record, whichever is written); **a record holding DYNAMIC LENGTH members under an explicit format 1 is carried in its FIXED FORM — every member at its maximum width, space padded, still exactly integer-1 bytes with no frame and no table (§3 D-FRA (vi), kb/Work PB1562)**. **Such a file records no record boundaries**: §9.1.7.2 makes a record sequential record's length "determined by any information the implementor may add to the record on the physical storage medium", and this format adds none, so a READ takes the next integer-1 bytes of the READING file description as the physical record — GnuCOBOL's fixed-length sequential format (CLAUDE.md rule 1; kb/Work PB1514). A physical record is therefore never longer than the reading description's fixed length, and it is shorter only at the end of the file: that final partial record is §9.1.13.2 item 3's '04' (§14.9.30.4 GR14 — the rest of the record area is undefined). A file written under another record length is read in the reading description's lengths (`conformance:2002/io_status_04`: three 5-byte records read as a 10-byte record and a 5-byte '04' record; one 15-byte record read the same way); `RecordLayoutNotice` reports a file whose length is not a whole number of records at OPEN, without changing the I-O status; **(b) record sequential, variable-length records** (format 2, format 3, or an implied format 2) — **4 + the record's own byte size**: a 4-byte little-endian length word before each record (item 151), plus `4 + 8 × k` bytes when the record is a variable-length group with k variable components (its extent table, D-FRA (v)); **(c) line sequential** — the record's characters less the trailing spaces §14.9.51.4 GR21 does not transfer (a file whose RECORD clause has the DEPENDING phrase is filled to data-name-1's length instead, GR22), one byte each, plus the line delimiter of item 114 (CR LF on Windows, LF on Linux and macOS); a record area holding a character outside the line sequential character set is not written at all ('71', item 115); **(c′) record sequential PRINT files** — a record sequential file written with an ADVANCING phrase or described with a LINAGE clause (and every report file) is a print stream, not framed records: each record is presented as its characters less trailing spaces (a variable-length record at its §13.18.43.4 GR13 length, and a WRITE whose length is outside the RECORD IS VARYING bounds presents nothing — '44', §14.9.51.4 GR14/GR15), one byte each, and the page is advanced with CR LF line controls and a form feed for ADVANCING PAGE, on every host (it is not a line sequential file, so item 114 does not govern it); no length word is written; **(d) relative and indexed** — the file begins with the store header (20 bytes plus each key's descriptor: the §9.1.6 fixed file attributes, item 129) and EVERY record, fixed-length or variable, is framed as in (b); an empty relative slot is the 4-byte tag `0xFFFFFFFF` alone. **CODE-SET** never changes a size: every claimed code set (the identity sets, and EBCDIC = CCSID 37) is one byte per alphanumeric character, and the framing in (b)–(d) stays native (A.3 item 27). The **FORMAT clause** cannot arise: A.4.8 is not claimed and a FORMAT clause is refused with COBOLNET1705 (A.1 items 84 and 85 cannot arise). | `conformance:85/pb1276_record_contains_sizes_file`; `conformance:2002/io_status_04`; `conformance:2023/pb1540_ls_lone_cr_is_data` |
